    3. Logos — argument's logical structure and evidence
  Description: Aristotle's three modes of persuasion. Not a sequential beat structure but a triad of registers any persuasive text combines.
  Exemplars: Lincoln's Gettysburg Address; MLK's "I Have a Dream"; great political speeches generally.

GENRE 134: Inverted Pyramid (journalism)
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Lead — who, what, when, where, why, how
    2. Important supporting details
    3. Quotes and context
    4. Background and tangential information
  Description: Defining structure of news writing — most important information first, supporting details next, background last. Designed for telegraph-era news.
  Exemplars: Wire-service news (AP, Reuters); breaking news in major newspapers; NYT front-page hard news.

GENRE 135: Nut-graf feature lead
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Anecdotal or scene-setting lead
    2. Nut graf — "this story is about [larger phenomenon]"
    3. Body — supporting reporting, examples, perspectives
    4. Closing kicker
  Description: Feature-style structure: anecdotal opening followed by paragraph explaining why the story matters. The reader is invited in by scene before being told the stakes.
  Exemplars: WSJ front-page features (canonical); New Yorker reportage; long-form magazine journalism.

GENRE 136: Tick-Tock
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Setup — the situation just before
    2. Hour-by-hour or moment-by-moment reconstruction
    3. Each scene moves time forward
    4. Climactic event reached at its actual chronological point
    5. Brief immediate aftermath
  Description: Reconstructed-chronology narrative. A major event retold beat-by-beat in real time, often using interviews and documents.
  Exemplars: WaPo tick-tocks of 9/11, Capitol riot, election nights; Politico post-election reconstructions.

GENRE 137: Profile / Portrait
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Opening scene — subject in characteristic action
    2. Initial telling detail
    3. Backstory and rise
    4. Current situation; what is at stake now
    5. Defining encounter or moment with the writer
    6. Closing image that crystallizes the subject
  Description: Journalistic biographical narrative of a single person. Present scene → backstory → present significance. Tied to a present news peg.
  Exemplars: Talese's "Frank Sinatra Has a Cold"; New Yorker profiles; Esquire profiles; Vanity Fair features.

GENRE 138: Long-form literary journalism
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Scene that opens with vivid specificity
    2. Cast of characters established
    3. Reportorial backstory and context interwoven
    4. Multiple scenes build the narrative arc
    5. Climactic event or revelation
    6. Closing scene that returns or transforms the opening
  Description: Extended reported nonfiction using techniques of fiction — scene-by-scene, full dialogue, interior perspective, status detail — to render real events.
  Exemplars: Capote's In Cold Blood; Didion's Slouching Towards Bethlehem; Wolfe's The Right Stuff; Orlean's The Orchid Thief.

GENRE 139: Investigative narrative
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Anomaly or tip surfaces
    2. Initial reporting raises questions
    3. Resistance from subjects encountered
    4. Documents and sources break the story open
    5. Pattern of wrongdoing established
    6. Confrontation and accountability sought
    7. Story published; consequences follow
  Description: Reported narrative whose engine is the journalist's investigation itself — gradual uncovering of hidden facts, often involving institutional wrongdoing.
  Exemplars: All the President's Men (Woodward-Bernstein); Spotlight (Boston Globe); Farrow's Catch and Kill; ProPublica.

GENRE 140: Case Study
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Company and situation introduced
    2. Decision point — what choice was faced
    3. Options considered
    4. Path taken
    5. Outcome
    6. Analysis — what made it work or fail
    7. Lessons applicable beyond this case
  Description: Structured narrative of specific business situation, decision, and outcome. Used in education, consulting, B2B marketing. Analytic register; narrative serves transferable lessons.
  Exemplars: Harvard Business School cases; McKinsey case studies; HubSpot customer cases; HBR case features.

GENRE 141: Pitch Deck Story
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Problem in the world
    2. Why now (market timing)
    3. Solution
    4. How it works (product)
    5. Market size and opportunity
    6. Traction or proof
    7. Team
    8. Ask
  Description: Narrative arc of a startup investor pitch. Engineered for 10-15 slides and 15 minutes. Establishes need and offer in tight time.
  Exemplars: YC pitch deck template; Sequoia template; Airbnb's 2008 pitch deck.

GENRE 142: Brand Origin Story
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Founder's pre-company life
    2. Specific moment of insight or unmet need
    3. Decision to pursue
    4. Early struggle or near-failure
    5. Breakthrough that validated the path
    6. Present-day significance
  Description: Company's foundational narrative — founder's moment of insight, unmet need perceived, early struggle, present significance. Creates emotional attachment to brand.
  Exemplars: Nike (Phil Knight's car); Apple's garage; Patagonia's Yvon Chouinard; Airbnb's air mattresses.

GENRE 143: Customer Success Story
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Customer's pre-product situation (the pain)
    2. Discovery of the product
    3. Implementation and early skepticism
    4. Specific moment of value realization
    5. Quantified outcome and present state
  Description: Narrative of specific customer's transformation through using a product. Before-state → catalyst → after-state, with measurable outcomes.
  Exemplars: Salesforce customer cases; nonprofit impact reports; HBR customer features.

GENRE 144: Crisis Communication
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Acknowledge the situation immediately and specifically
    2. Express empathy for those affected
    3. Take responsibility (where appropriate)
    4. Explain what is being done now
    5. Explain what will prevent recurrence
    6. Commit to ongoing transparency
  Description: Structured corporate response to scandal, accident, or public failure. Acknowledge harm, accept responsibility, articulate corrective action, restore trust.
  Exemplars: J&J Tylenol response (1982 gold standard); Domino's 2009 video response; Boeing 737 MAX (negative example).

GENRE 145: Disruption Narrative
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Incumbent dominance established
    2. Disruptor enters with seemingly inferior product
    3. Incumbent dismisses the threat
    4. Disruptor improves rapidly along an unexpected dimension
    5. Market redefined; incumbent wrong-footed
    6. Present-day market reorganized around disruptor
  Description: Christensen's framing as public narrative: incumbent industry leader overtaken by smaller competitor offering "worse" product that redefines market.
  Exemplars: Netflix vs Blockbuster; Tesla vs legacy auto; Uber vs taxis; Airbnb vs hotels; Christensen's The Innovator's Dilemma.

GENRE 146: White Paper
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Executive summary
    2. Problem definition
    3. Current state of solutions and their limitations
    4. Proposed approach
    5. Detailed analysis and evidence
    6. Implementation considerations
    7. Conclusion and call to action
  Description: Long-form authoritative document defining a problem, examining existing solutions, advocating a specific approach. Used in B2B marketing, policy advocacy.
  Exemplars: Government policy white papers; Bitcoin whitepaper (Satoshi 2008); enterprise software vendor whitepapers.

GENRE 147: IMRAD Research Paper
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Introduction — problem, prior work, hypothesis
    2. Methods — how the research was conducted
    3. Results — what was found
    4. Discussion — what the results mean, limitations, implications
  Description: Standard structure of empirical scientific papers. Designed for reproducibility and rapid expert reading.
  Exemplars: Most empirical scientific papers since mid-20th c.; Nature, Science, biomedical journals.

GENRE 148: Literature Review
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Topic and scope defined
    2. Search and inclusion criteria stated
    3. Major theoretical frameworks summarized
    4. Empirical findings organized thematically
    5. Contested questions and tensions identified
    6. Gaps in current literature
    7. Implications for future research
  Description: Academic synthesis of existing research — a structured map of the field. Identifies major findings, contested questions, methodological approaches, gaps.
  Exemplars: Annual Review articles; doctoral dissertation literature-review chapters; systematic reviews in medicine.

GENRE 149: Position Paper / Op-Ed
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Hook — a vivid case or news peg
    2. Position stated clearly
    3. Stakes — why it matters now
    4. Supporting argument with evidence
    5. Anticipation of counterarguments
    6. Call to action or implication
  Description: Short-to-medium argumentative essay advocating a specific position. Compressed for journalistic or institutional reading.
  Exemplars: NYT op-eds; Atlantic essays; institutional position papers; academic policy essays.

GENRE 150: Grant Proposal
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Project summary / abstract
    2. Statement of need / problem
    3. Goals and objectives
    4. Methodology / approach
    5. Evaluation plan
    6. Team qualifications
    7. Budget and budget justification
    8. Sustainability plan
  Description: Structured request for funding research, programs, or projects. Distinguished by dual audience: program officers and reviewers.
  Exemplars: NIH R01 grants; NSF proposals; foundation grants (Ford, MacArthur, Gates); humanities NEH grants.

GENRE 151: Opening Statement (legal)
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Theme of the case stated in one sentence
    2. Brief story of what happened
    3. Identification of key witnesses and what they will say
    4. Promise of what the evidence will show
    5. What the lawyer will ask the jury to find
  Description: First address by lawyer to jury, previewing the case. Strictly bounded — must be roadmap, not argument; promises evidence rather than asserting conclusions.
  Exemplars: Iconic opening statements: O.J. Simpson trial; Atticus Finch in To Kill a Mockingbird; A Few Good Men.

GENRE 152: Closing Argument (legal)
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Restatement of the theme
    2. Story of what happened, now with evidentiary backing
    3. Why the evidence proves the claim
    4. Anticipation and rebuttal of opposing argument
    5. Specific verdict requested
  Description: Lawyer's final address to jury, connecting evidence to verdict requested. Argument is permitted; lawyer is now persuading rather than previewing.
  Exemplars: Atticus Finch's closing in To Kill a Mockingbird; Clarence Darrow's closings; 12 Angry Men.

GENRE 153: Stump Speech
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Local greeting / acknowledgment
    2. Personal story that grounds the candidate
    3. Diagnosis of what is wrong
    4. Vision of what could be
    5. Specific policy commitments
    6. Call to action — vote, volunteer, donate
  Description: Repeated political speech delivered across many campaign stops. Modular and emotionally consistent — same emotional arc through varying details.
  Exemplars: Obama's 2008 campaign stump; Bernie Sanders 2016/2020; classical campaign speeches.

GENRE 154: Inaugural Address
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Acknowledgment of the moment and predecessors
    2. Naming of the historical challenge or opportunity
    3. Invocation of shared national values
    4. Vision and call to collective effort
    5. Ceremonial close — often invoking divine or historical witness
  Description: Ceremonial speech by incoming head of state. Distinguished by move from contested partisanship to symbolic unity.
  Exemplars: Lincoln's Second Inaugural; FDR's First Inaugural; JFK's "ask not"; Obama 2009.

GENRE 155: Concession Speech
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Acknowledgment of the loss
    2. Congratulation of the winner
    3. Recognition of the campaign's supporters
    4. Affirmation of shared democratic values
    5. Forward-looking commitment to civic engagement
  Description: Speech of defeated candidate acknowledging loss and legitimacy of opponent's victory. The transfer of legitimacy IS the engine.
  Exemplars: Al Gore 2000; John McCain 2008; Hillary Clinton 2016.

GENRE 156: Manifesto
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Diagnosis of the broken present
    2. Identification of the responsible parties or systems
    3. Declaration of principles
    4. Vision of the new order
    5. Call to immediate action or alignment
  Description: Declarative public statement of intent or principles by a movement. Designed to provoke alignment and rupture. Galvanizes rather than persuades.
  Exemplars: Marx and Engels's Communist Manifesto; Futurist Manifesto (Marinetti); Port Huron Statement; Haraway's Cyborg Manifesto.

GENRE 157: Whistleblower Narrative
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Insider's position and initial belief in the institution
    2. Discovery of wrongdoing
    3. Internal reporting attempted and rebuffed
    4. Decision to go public
    5. Personal cost paid
    6. Implications for the institution and the public
  Description: First-person account of insider exposing institutional wrongdoing, often at personal cost. Authority comes from inside position.
  Exemplars: Snowden's Permanent Record; Ellsberg's Secrets; Frances Haugen; Wigand (basis for The Insider).

GENRE 158: Cosmic Horror (Lovecraftian)
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Scholar or investigator encounters disturbing artifact or text
    2. Investigation reveals fragments of cosmic truth
    3. Each new revelation is worse than the last
    4. Glimpse of the actual cosmic horror
    5. Investigator's mind cracks under the truth
    6. Closing — suggestion that the horror persists
  Description: Horror based on smallness of human consciousness against indifferent or malevolent cosmos. The threat is ontological, not personal.
  Exemplars: Lovecraft's "Call of Cthulhu," "At the Mountains of Madness"; Blackwood's "The Willows"; True Detective S1; Annihilation (VanderMeer).

GENRE 159: Gothic Horror
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Innocent protagonist arrives at gloomy ancestral place
    2. Strange occurrences accumulate
    3. Family secret hinted at
    4. Confrontation with the buried past
    5. Climactic revelation, often with violence
    6. House or estate destroyed or transformed
  Description: Horror anchored in atmospheric place — haunted house, ruined castle, decaying estate — and family secrets, suppressed sexuality, return of the buried past.
  Exemplars: Walpole's Castle of Otranto; Wuthering Heights; Jane Eyre; Rebecca; The Haunting of Hill House; Crimson Peak.

GENRE 160: Whodunit
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Murder discovered in a contained setting
    2. Detective arrives; suspects introduced
    3. Investigation; clues planted
    4. False solutions explored and disproven
    5. Detective gathers all suspects
    6. Reveal of the true murderer with reasoning
  Description: Puzzle-mystery in which detective gathers clues to identify killer from closed cast of suspects. The pleasure is the deduction; clues are fairly available to careful reader.
  Exemplars: Christie's And Then There Were None, Murder on the Orient Express; Conan Doyle's Sherlock Holmes; Knives Out; Glass Onion.

GENRE 161: Hardboiled Detective
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Detective hired for a seemingly small case
    2. First-person narrator establishes voice and city
    3. Case opens up — wider corruption hinted
    4. Beatings, betrayals, ambiguous women
    5. Climactic confrontation
    6. Resolution that does not restore order — corruption persists
  Description: Detective story rooted in urban realism, moral ambiguity, cynical first-person narrator. The puzzle is secondary to atmosphere and detective's character.
  Exemplars: Hammett's The Maltese Falcon; Chandler's The Big Sleep; Mosley's Easy Rawlins; Chinatown; Mystic River.

GENRE 162: Cozy Mystery
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Cozy setting and community established
    2. Murder occurs (often off-page)
    3. Amateur detective begins investigating
    4. Suspects are community members
    5. Detective uses local knowledge to solve
    6. Murderer revealed; community restored
  Description: Puzzle-mystery in low-violence pastoral or small-town setting with amateur detective. The world is one the reader wants to return to.
  Exemplars: Christie's Miss Marple; Murder, She Wrote; Hamish Macbeth; Only Murders in the Building.

GENRE 163: Bildungsroman
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Childhood — protagonist's inherited circumstance
    2. Departure from home into wider world
    3. Sequence of formative encounters and lessons
    4. First serious love
    5. Disillusionment or crisis
    6. Re-orientation and decision about life path
    7. Settled adult identity
  Description: European novel of formation. Young protagonist's psychological and moral development from naïve youth through formative trials to mature adulthood.
  Exemplars: Goethe's Wilhelm Meister's Apprenticeship; David Copperfield; Great Expectations; Portrait of the Artist; To Kill a Mockingbird.

GENRE 164: Picaresque
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Pícaro introduced as low-born and resourceful
    2. Picaresque journey begins — series of episodic encounters
    3. Each episode exposes a different social stratum's hypocrisy
    4. Pícaro's wits are tested and survive
    5. Closing — pícaro continues onward, unchanged
  Description: Satirical episodic novel following low-born rogue through adventures across various social strata, exposing each through the rogue's wits. Pícaro does NOT mature — they survive.
  Exemplars: Lazarillo de Tormes; Don Quixote (in part); Tom Jones; Huckleberry Finn; Augie March; Catch-22.

---

# NGC ORIGINAL GENRES

GENRE 165: Refusal-of-the-Easy
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Easy and obvious gain offered
    2. Quiet refusal that needs no defense
    3. Cost of refusal absorbed without complaint
  Description: Short narrative whose entire arc is the protagonist's deliberate refusal of an obviously beneficial choice — not because they fail to see its value, but because accepting would betray something they refuse to name. Quiet sovereignty as the resolution.
  Exemplars: Cincinnatus narratives; Washington stepping down after two terms; Bartleby's "I would prefer not to" extended into a structural arc.

GENRE 166: The Inversion of Care
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Devoted care established as a relationship's defining trait
    2. The care, taken to an extreme or wrong shape, harms its object
    3. Recognition that the love and the harm cannot be separated
  Description: Narrative in which the act of caring for someone causes the harm it sought to prevent. Captures the loving harmer — good intentions becoming the wound.
  Exemplars: Lady Bird (mother's love as criticism); helicopter-parenting narratives; over-protection that accelerates decline.

GENRE 167: Witness-Without-Intervention
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Protagonist arrives in position to witness
    2. Sustained, attentive observation of what unfolds
    3. Departure — what was seen is now carried by the witness
  Description: Narrative in which the protagonist is positioned as witness to something significant — usually injustice or beauty — and the structural payoff is the quality of the witnessing itself, not action taken.
  Exemplars: Travel writing; quieter wartime journalism; Didion's California essays; certain photography essays (Salgado).

GENRE 168: Slow-Yes
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Polite, unforced no held
    2. Series of small offerings absorbed without acknowledgment
    3. Yes arrives quietly, almost without ceremony
  Description: Narrative whose entire arc is the gradual softening of a no into a yes — across many small moments rather than a single decisive turn. Cumulative arrival without obstacle.
  Exemplars: Late-life-romance narratives; mentor/student arcs; The Remains of the Day (in some readings); Lost in Translation.

GENRE 169: Ledger-Closes
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Old, unsettled obligation established as background
    2. Quiet decision and movement to settle it now
    3. Settlement made; ledger closed
  Description: Narrative whose engine is settling a long-standing moral debt — not revenge (hot, proportional), but cool deliberate return of what was owed, after many years.
  Exemplars: Restitution narratives in post-war fiction; The Reader (Schlink); Ishiguro's The Buried Giant; estate-and-inheritance dramas.

GENRE 170: The Held Question
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Initial question raised, perhaps casually
    2. Partial answers and complications deepen the question
    3. Question articulated more precisely; resolution declined
  Description: Narrative whose payoff is the deepening of a question rather than its resolution. The question itself ripens. Reader leaves more curious, more precisely uncertain.
  Exemplars: Borges's "The Library of Babel"; Anne Carson's The Glass Essay; The Crying of Lot 49 (Pynchon); some W.G. Sebald.

---

# UNIVERSAL — PLOT ARCHETYPES & RHETORICAL FORMS

GENRE 171: Chosen One narrative
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Ordinary life with hidden marker
    2. Prophecy or sign reveals the chosen status
    3. Refusal or doubt
    4. Acceptance of fate
    5. Trials that prove or threaten the chosenness
    6. Final confrontation only the chosen can face
  Description: Protagonist singled out by prophecy, birth, or supernatural sign as uniquely fated. Distinct from Hero's Journey by its external selection — the call is not refuseable in any meaningful way.
  Exemplars: Harry Potter; The Matrix (Neo); Star Wars (Anakin/Luke); Dune (Paul Atreides); Buffy the Vampire Slayer.

GENRE 172: Fish Out of Water
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Protagonist established in their native element
    2. Sudden relocation to alien environment
    3. Failures of adaptation produce comedy or trouble
    4. Gradual learning or unexpected competence
    5. Resolution — protagonist either adapts or returns transformed
  Description: Protagonist placed in environment radically alien to their habits and competencies; the friction between protagonist and setting drives both comedy and growth.
  Exemplars: Crocodile Dundee; Coming to America; Lost in Translation; Schitt's Creek.

GENRE 173: Mentor and Pupil
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Pupil's need or potential established
    2. Mentor encountered, often reluctantly
    3. Training or teaching begins
    4. Pupil's trial (with or without mentor present)
    5. Mentor's lesson tested against reality
    6. Pupil surpasses, honors, or buries the mentor
  Description: Narrative centered on relationship between experienced master and inexperienced student. The mentor's wisdom is tested as much as the pupil's capacity. Story is about transmission across generations.
  Exemplars: Star Wars (Yoda/Luke); Karate Kid; Good Will Hunting; Dead Poets Society; Whiplash.

GENRE 174: Buddy narrative
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Both characters established in their distinct modes
    2. Forced pairing
    3. Initial friction and incompatibility
    4. Mutual adjustment under pressure
    5. Trust forged through trial
    6. Closing affirmation of the bond
  Description: Two protagonists with contrasting temperaments, methods, or backgrounds paired (often forced together). Pleasure is the friction-and-bond between mismatched partners.
  Exemplars: Lethal Weapon; Rush Hour; Toy Story; Pulp Fiction (Vincent and Jules); 48 Hrs.

GENRE 175: Ensemble narrative
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Cast introduced in their network of relations
    2. Inciting situation affects the group
    3. Each member's response traced
    4. Lines of conflict and alliance form
    5. Climactic event involves the whole group
    6. New equilibrium for the ensemble
  Description: Narrative organized around a group of roughly equal-weight characters whose intersecting arcs constitute the story. The cast IS the story.
  Exemplars: The Big Chill; Magnolia; Crash; Love Actually; Friends; The Office.

GENRE 176: Stranger Comes to Town
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Community in stable equilibrium
    2. Stranger arrives
    3. Stranger's presence disturbs the equilibrium
    4. Hidden tensions in the community surface
    5. Confrontation with what the stranger represents
    6. Community transformed or stranger expelled
  Description: One of John Gardner's two foundational stories. Outsider's arrival disrupts static order of a community, exposing tensions and forcing reconfiguration.
  Exemplars: Shane; High Noon; The Stranger (Camus); Chocolat; Pale Rider.

GENRE 177: Hero Goes on a Journey
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Protagonist embedded in familiar life
    2. Decision or compulsion to leave
    3. Departure
    4. Encounters along the road
    5. Furthest point reached
    6. Return (or refusal of return)
  Description: Gardner's other foundational story. Protagonist leaves the familiar and travels into the unknown. The journey is the form, not necessarily a search.
  Exemplars: On the Road; The Wizard of Oz; Wild (Strayed); The Odyssey; many road-movie narratives.

GENRE 178: Sacrificial Lamb
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Innocent figure introduced as community's hope or treasure
    2. Threat to community emerges
    3. Innocent's vulnerability becomes the crisis
    4. Community's survival requires their loss
    5. Loss occurs
    6. Community reconstituted around the absence
  Description: Innocent character must die or suffer to redeem or save a community, often without their full understanding of why. The lamb may not even understand the role.
  Exemplars: The Lottery (Jackson); Sophie's Choice; certain biblical narratives; Of Mice and Men (Lennie).

GENRE 179: Riches to Rags
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Wealth, comfort, or position enjoyed
    2. First crack — error, betrayal, or external shock
    3. Decline accelerates; possessions and status stripped
    4. Bottom reached — destitution or ruin
    5. Reckoning with the new reality
  Description: Mirror of Booker's Rags-to-Riches. Character begins in wealth, status, or comfort and falls — through error, betrayal, or systemic collapse — into deprivation.
  Exemplars: King Lear; Joseph Roth's Job; Margin Call (corporate variant); Great Depression narratives; Falling Down.

GENRE 180: Round Robin / Interlocking narrative
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. First story-arc introduced
    2. Second story-arc with passing reference to the first
    3. Third arc — pattern of connection emerges
    4. Climactic convergence or thematic crystallization
    5. Closing — pattern revealed
  Description: Narrative composed of multiple discrete stories that connect through shared characters, locations, themes, or coincidences. The reader's pleasure is recognition of connections.
  Exemplars: Pulp Fiction; Magnolia; Cloud Atlas; A Visit from the Goon Squad (Egan); Olive Kitteridge.

---

# CLASSICAL RHETORICAL MODES (the 9 modes of discourse)

GENRE 181: Narration mode
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Setting and characters established
    2. Events occur in sequence
    3. Climactic moment
    4. Resolution
  Description: Telling a story — sequential events arranged in time. The fundamental rhetorical mode. Use when the goal is to recount what happened.
  Exemplars: Most fiction; personal essays; news reports.

GENRE 182: Description mode
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Subject named
    2. Spatial or sensory orientation
    3. Layered detail (visual, auditory, tactile, olfactory)
    4. Telling specifics that establish character
    5. Closing image or impression
  Description: Detailed sensory rendering of a person, place, object, or scene. The goal is to make the reader perceive. Use when content is what something is like rather than what happens.
  Exemplars: Travel writing; nature writing (Annie Dillard); descriptive passages in literary fiction.

GENRE 183: Exposition mode
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Topic introduced
    2. Key terms defined
    3. Principles explained
    4. Examples or applications
    5. Synthesis or conclusion
  Description: Explanation of an idea, concept, or process. The goal is to make the reader understand. Use when content is informational and the reader needs to grasp how something works.
  Exemplars: Most explanatory journalism; textbook prose; Wikipedia articles; popular-science writing.

GENRE 184: Argumentation mode
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Claim stated
    2. Reasons advanced
    3. Evidence presented
    4. Counterarguments addressed
    5. Conclusion drawn
  Description: Reasoning to convince. The mode designed for changing minds. Use when the goal is to advance a contested claim with evidence and reasoning.
  Exemplars: Op-eds; legal briefs; academic argumentative essays.

GENRE 185: Comparison and Contrast
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Two or more subjects introduced
    2. Common ground established
    3. Point-by-point contrast or similarity
    4. Synthesis — what the comparison reveals
  Description: Structure organized around the systematic juxtaposition of two or more subjects. Use when understanding comes through what is like and what is unlike.
  Exemplars: "X vs Y" essays; product comparisons; literary criticism comparing authors.

GENRE 186: Cause and Effect
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Phenomenon to be explained named
    2. Background conditions described
    3. Causes traced (proximate and ultimate)
    4. Effects unfolded
    5. Implications drawn
  Description: Structure tracing causal chains — what produces what, and why. Use when content is why something happened or what something will produce.
  Exemplars: Historical analysis; epidemiological reports; many Atlantic explanatory features.

GENRE 187: Process Analysis
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Goal or end-state of the process named
    2. Materials, prerequisites, or starting conditions
    3. Sequenced steps
    4. Common pitfalls or variations
    5. Final state achieved
  Description: Step-by-step explanation of how something is done or how something happens. Two sub-modes: directive (instructions) and informative. Use when content is procedural.
  Exemplars: Recipes; instructional manuals; Wikihow articles; how-to journalism.

GENRE 188: Definition mode
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Term named
    2. Genus established (the larger category)
    3. Differentia (what distinguishes it from others in the genus)
    4. Examples and counter-examples
    5. Refinement or boundary discussion
  Description: Establishing precisely what a term, concept, or category means — by genus, by negation, by example, by etymology. Use when content's success depends on a term being shared.
  Exemplars: Dictionary entries; philosophical essays defining terms; scientific definitions.

GENRE 189: Classification and Division
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Domain to be classified named
    2. Principle of division stated
    3. Categories enumerated
    4. Each category defined and exemplified
    5. Boundary cases discussed
  Description: Organizing a complex domain into categories (classification) or breaking a single thing into its parts (division). Use when content is taxonomic.
  Exemplars: Linnaean biological taxonomy; this very catalog; diagnostic frameworks (DSM-5).

---

# PERSUASION STRUCTURES (logical and informal)

GENRE 190: Syllogism
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Major premise
    2. Minor premise
    3. Conclusion
  Description: Aristotle's three-part deductive argument. The foundational form of formal reasoning.
  Exemplars: "All men are mortal; Socrates is a man; therefore Socrates is mortal."

GENRE 191: Enthymeme
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Stated premise
    2. Stated premise OR conclusion (one missing)
    3. Inference completed by audience
  Description: Syllogism with one premise unstated — assumed shared with the audience. The most common form of real-world persuasive reasoning.
  Exemplars: "He's a politician — of course he's lying"; most editorial argumentation.

GENRE 192: Argument by Analogy
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Case A described
    2. Case B (the one in question) described
    3. Relevant features shared
    4. Conclusion: what holds in A likely holds in B
  Description: Reasoning that two cases share enough relevant features that what is true of one is likely true of the other. Use when direct evidence is absent but a comparable case can be invoked.
  Exemplars: Legal precedent argument; "Inviting them is like inviting a fox into the henhouse"; many policy debates.

GENRE 193: Confirmation-Refutation
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Statement of position
    2. Confirmation — positive arguments for your view
    3. Refutation — dismantling of opposing view
    4. Conclusion
  Description: Classical rhetorical structure: first establish your own arguments positively, then dismantle the opposing view. Use when audience is mixed and both sides need addressing.
  Exemplars: Cicero's speeches; classical legal argumentation; many traditional sermons.

GENRE 194: Concession-and-Counter
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Acknowledge the opposing point as valid
    2. Specify the limits of that validity
    3. Pivot to your stronger point
    4. Conclude with refined position
  Description: A move within argument: explicitly grant a point to the opposing view, then redirect to a stronger point that holds despite the concession. Honest acknowledgment of partial agreement strengthens credibility.
  Exemplars: "Yes, the policy has costs — but the costs of inaction are higher"; common in skilled op-eds.

GENRE 195: Reductio ad Absurdum
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Opposing premise stated
    2. Logical extension carried out
    3. Absurd or contradictory consequence reached
    4. Premise rejected
  Description: Showing that an opponent's premise leads to absurd or contradictory consequences, thereby disproving the premise.
  Exemplars: Plato's dialogues; satirical essays (Swift's "A Modest Proposal"); legal argument.

---

# PROPAGANDA / INFLUENCE FORMS (Institute for Propaganda Analysis, 1937)

GENRE 196: Bandwagon
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Crowd's behavior or belief asserted
    2. Implication that joining is natural or inevitable
    3. Call to align with the majority
  Description: Persuasion arguing "everyone is doing it / believes it / supports it" — therefore you should join. Exploits social proof.
  Exemplars: Election advertising; consumer marketing ("Join the millions...").

GENRE 197: Glittering Generality
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Virtuous-sounding term invoked
    2. Association with the proposition
    3. Audience completes the meaning favorably
  Description: Use of vague, virtuous-sounding terms (freedom, justice, family values) without specific content — letting the audience fill in their own meaning.
  Exemplars: Most political speech; brand slogans; nationalist rhetoric.

GENRE 198: Card Stacking
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Position to be supported
    2. Selective evidence assembled
    3. Contradictory evidence omitted
    4. Position presented as if comprehensively supported
  Description: Selective presentation of evidence — only the facts that support the position, omitting contradictory evidence.
  Exemplars: Advertising; tendentious journalism; legal briefs (within ethical bounds).

GENRE 199: Plain Folks appeal
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Speaker's elite or unusual status known
    2. Speaker performs ordinary register and concerns
    3. Identification fostered
    4. Position offered as folk wisdom
  Description: Speaker positions themselves as ordinary, just-like-you, to bypass elite-skepticism in the audience.
  Exemplars: Political speeches by candidates of privilege; folksy CEO messaging; populist communication.

GENRE 200: Testimonial appeal
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Trusted figure introduced
    2. Their endorsement of position presented
    3. Transfer of trust to proposition
  Description: A respected or admired figure endorses the position or product. Audience trust in a third party transfers to the proposition.
  Exemplars: Celebrity ads; expert endorsements; political endorsements.

GENRE 201: Transfer
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Loaded symbol introduced
    2. Proposition placed alongside it
    3. Audience transfers feeling
  Description: Associating the proposition with a symbol, image, or institution that the audience already values (flag, religion, family) — transferring positive feeling.
  Exemplars: Patriotic political ads; brand storytelling that invokes religious imagery; nostalgic marketing.

GENRE 202: Name Calling
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Opposition introduced
    2. Pejorative label applied
    3. Audience encouraged to dismiss without examining
  Description: Discrediting the opposing position by attaching pejorative labels — without engaging the substance. Recognized propaganda technique; ethically loaded.
  Exemplars: Political mudslinging; ad hominem attacks; ideological labeling.

GENRE 203: Fear Appeal
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Threat established
    2. Consequences vivid
    3. Solution offered (the proposition)
    4. Action urged before fear subsides
  Description: Persuasion grounded in arousing fear of consequences if audience does not adopt the position. Powerful and ethically heavy.
  Exemplars: Public-health campaigns (smoking, drunk driving); insurance ads; political fear campaigns.

---

# ADDITIONAL JOURNALISM GENRES

GENRE 204: Editorial (institutional voice)
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Issue defined
    2. Editorial board's position
    3. Reasoning and evidence
    4. Specific call to action
    5. Closing institutional declaration
  Description: Unsigned argumentative piece representing the position of a publication's editorial board. Distinguished from op-eds by institutional weight.
  Exemplars: New York Times editorials; Wall Street Journal editorials; The Economist leaders.

GENRE 205: Letter to the Editor
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Reference to original article
    2. Position
    3. One or two pointed reasons
    4. Closing return to the larger stake
  Description: Short, signed reader response to previous coverage. 200-400 words. Conversational claim on shared readership.
  Exemplars: Daily newspaper letters pages; London Review of Books letters; New York Review of Books letters.

GENRE 206: Obituary
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Death announcement (with cause and date)
    2. Life's distinctive achievement
    3. Background and rise
    4. Defining episodes
    5. Survivors and legacy
  Description: Biographical narrative of a recently-deceased person. Valedictory mode — what was this life for.
  Exemplars: New York Times obituaries; The Economist's Monday obit; Telegraph's eccentric obituaries.

GENRE 207: Sports Feature
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Vivid game or athlete moment
    2. Athlete's backstory
    3. Stakes of the present moment
    4. Defining play or decision
    5. What the result means beyond sport
  Description: Long-form sports narrative going beyond game results to character, context, and meaning. The sports event or athlete carries broader human or cultural weight.
  Exemplars: Sports Illustrated features; The Players' Tribune; W.C. Heinz; Gary Smith.

GENRE 208: Service Journalism / How-to
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Reader's problem named
    2. Stakes acknowledged
    3. Steps with rationale
    4. Pitfalls and variations
    5. Outcome described
  Description: Practical instructional content presented in journalistic form. Blends process analysis with editorial selection.
  Exemplars: Wirecutter product guides; Lifehacker how-tos; women's-magazine service features.

GENRE 209: News Analysis
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Recent event described
    2. Context and history given
    3. Stakeholders' positions
    4. Likely consequences
    5. What to watch next
  Description: Signed journalist's interpretation of news events — distinguishing implications, providing context, identifying stakes. Distinct from news (reports) and op-ed (argues): analysis explains and frames.
  Exemplars: NYT "News Analysis" pieces; Politico analysis pieces; Atlantic explanatory journalism.

GENRE 210: Listicle
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Hook (the count)
    2. Item 1 with detail
    3. Item 2
    4. Successive items
    5. Closing image or kicker
  Description: Information presented as a numbered list with brief descriptions. Scannable structure: each item is self-contained.
  Exemplars: BuzzFeed lists; Cracked features; many magazine year-end lists.

---

# ADDITIONAL BUSINESS / STRATEGY GENRES

GENRE 211: Annual Letter to Shareholders
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Year's headline result
    2. Strategic context
    3. What worked and what didn't
    4. Lessons
    5. Forward-looking vision
    6. Personal closing
  Description: CEO's signed reflection on the year's performance, strategy, and vision. Hybrid: financial accountability + brand storytelling + strategic communication.
  Exemplars: Warren Buffett's Berkshire letters; Jeff Bezos's Amazon shareholder letters; Howard Schultz's Starbucks letters.

GENRE 212: Quarterly Earnings Call narrative
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. CEO opens with strategic frame
    2. CFO walks numbers
    3. Highlights and challenges acknowledged
    4. Forward guidance
    5. Q&A
    6. Closing summary
  Description: Structured corporate narrative delivered to analysts and investors quarterly. Engineered for credibility under scrutiny.
  Exemplars: Apple, Microsoft, Tesla earnings calls; SEC-required communications.

GENRE 213: SWOT-Style Strategic Narrative
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Strategic question framed
    2. Strengths
    3. Weaknesses
    4. Opportunities
    5. Threats
    6. Synthesis with recommended direction
  Description: Strategic analysis organized as Strengths / Weaknesses / Opportunities / Threats — internal and external, positive and negative.
  Exemplars: Business school cases; consulting deliverables; corporate strategy reviews.

GENRE 214: OKR Story
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Objective stated
    2. Why it matters
    3. Key results defined
    4. Progress against each KR
    5. Lessons
    6. Next-cycle objectives
  Description: Strategic narrative organized around Objectives (qualitative goals) and Key Results (quantitative measures). Used at Intel, Google, and across modern tech.
  Exemplars: Google internal reviews; modern tech company quarterly reviews; Measure What Matters (Doerr).

GENRE 215: Post-Mortem / Retrospective
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Incident summary
    2. Timeline of events
    3. Root cause analysis
    4. Contributing factors
    5. What went well
    6. Action items for future
  Description: Structured analysis of a project, incident, or failure after the fact, with the explicit goal of organizational learning rather than blame.
  Exemplars: Engineering post-mortems (Google SRE tradition); aviation incident reports; agile retrospectives.

---

# ADDITIONAL ACADEMIC GENRES

GENRE 216: Conference Paper / Talk
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Hook with question or stakes
    2. Background and prior work
    3. Methodology
    4. Results
    5. Implications
    6. Closing with call for questions
  Description: Short academic presentation (typically 15-20 minutes) at a conference. Compressed argument oriented toward in-room peer scrutiny.
  Exemplars: Most academic conference talks; AAAS, AMA, MLA conferences; academic colloquia.

GENRE 217: Dissertation Defense
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Research question
    2. Theoretical framework
    3. Methodology
    4. Findings
    5. Contributions
    6. Limitations
    7. Future research
    8. Q&A
  Description: Doctoral candidate's oral defense of their dissertation before a committee. High-stakes mastery demonstration under expert challenge.
  Exemplars: PhD dissertation defenses across disciplines; standard academic credentialing.

GENRE 218: Academic Book Review
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Book and field situated
    2. Argument summarized fairly
    3. Strengths assessed
    4. Weaknesses identified
    5. Place in disciplinary conversation
    6. Recommendation
  Description: Scholarly review of an academic book, situating it within a field and assessing contributions. Emphasizes disciplinary conversation.
  Exemplars: American Historical Review book reviews; Journal of Philosophy reviews; field-specific academic journals.

---

# MYTH & CREATION

GENRE 219: Creation myth (cosmogony)
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Primordial state (chaos, void, primal waters)
    2. First differentiation (light/dark, sky/earth)
    3. Emergence of cosmic order
    4. Creation of beings (gods, humans)
    5. Establishment of fundamental relationships
  Description: Narrative explaining how the world, universe, or fundamental order came to be. Establishes the most basic terms of reality.
  Exemplars: Genesis 1-2; Enuma Elish (Babylonian); Hesiod's Theogony; Diné creation; Yoruba Olódùmarè narratives.

GENRE 220: Flood myth
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Humanity in moral decline
    2. Divine decision to destroy
    3. Chosen survivor warned
    4. Preparations made (ark, vessel, mountain)
    5. Flood arrives
    6. Survivors emerge
    7. Covenant or new beginning established
  Description: Widely-distributed narrative type. Divine displeasure leads to a great flood; chosen few survive to rebuild. Found in Mesopotamian, Hebrew, Greek, Hindu, Aztec traditions.
  Exemplars: Genesis 6-9 (Noah); Epic of Gilgamesh (Utnapishtim); Greek Deucalion; Hindu Manu; Aztec Coxcoxtli.

---

GENRE 221: Theogony
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Primordial divine principle
    2. First gods emerge
    3. Generation of children gods
    4. Conflict between generations (often: child overthrows parent)
    5. Establishment of current divine order
    6. Distribution of cosmic offices
  Description: Narrative of the origin and genealogy of the gods. Distinguished from cosmogony by its specific concern with how the divine pantheon came into being and stratified.
  Exemplars: Hesiod's Theogony; Norse Eddic god-genealogies; Hurrian-Hittite Kumarbi cycle; Sumerian Enuma Elish.

GENRE 222: Hero-Birth narrative
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Distinguished parents
    2. Difficult conception or birth (often divine)
    3. Prophecy of greatness or threat to authority
    4. Authority's attempt to destroy infant
    5. Exposure/abandonment
    6. Rescue and raising elsewhere
    7. Eventual recognition and accession
  Description: Otto Rank's identified pattern. Hero's birth involves miraculous circumstances, attempt to destroy infant, exposure, rescue and raising by humble or animal foster-parents, eventual recognition.
  Exemplars: Moses; Romulus and Remus; Oedipus; Krishna; Sargon of Akkad; Cyrus the Great.

GENRE 223: Trickster Origin Cycle
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Trickster's appetite or desire
    2. Scheme launched
    3. Violation of cosmic norm
    4. Consequence as world-feature
    5. Continuation onward to next adventure
  Description: Pan-cultural narrative pattern. Trickster figure (Coyote, Anansi, Loki, Maui, Hermes, Eshu) wanders early world breaking rules; byproduct of misadventures is origin of present-world features.
  Exemplars: Anansi tales (Akan); Coyote tales (Plains/Southwest); Maui tales (Polynesian); Loki tales (Norse); Hermes (Greek).

GENRE 224: Apocalyptic / End-Times narrative
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Signs of the coming end
    2. Witness or prophet recognizes
    3. Forces of dissolution gather
    4. Final battle or judgment
    5. Old order destroyed
    6. New order or rebirth (in some traditions)
  Description: Narrative of the foretold or imminent end of an era, world, or order — often involving cosmic battle, judgment, and renewal. Ultimate stakes; structural payoff is reckoning.
  Exemplars: Book of Revelation; Norse Ragnarök; Hindu Kali Yuga / Kalki; Aztec Five Suns; Daniel and 2 Esdras.

---

# ORAL NARRATIVE FORMS

GENRE 225: Anecdote
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Setup — when and where
    2. Single incident
    3. Punctuating moment or insight
  Description: Short narrative of a single incident, usually personal, recounted to make a point, illustrate a character, or generate laughter. The shortest narrative form.
  Exemplars: Conversational storytelling; speeches that hinge on a single illustrating tale; many social-media micro-stories.

GENRE 226: Joke (3-beat)
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Setup — establishes premise
    2. Build — second iteration that confirms expectation
    3. Punchline — third iteration that subverts expectation
  Description: Setup, escalation, punchline. The classical compressed comedy form. Use when laughter is the entire purpose and structural economy is key.
  Exemplars: "A priest, a rabbi, and a minister walk into a bar..."; standup comedy; rule-of-three jokes.

GENRE 227: Tall Tale
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Plausible opening
    2. First exaggeration
    3. Each successive claim larger
    4. Climactic impossibility
    5. Deadpan close
  Description: Story whose comedy comes from progressive exaggeration — each detail more impossibly large than the last. Often featuring legendary folk heroes (Paul Bunyan, Pecos Bill).
  Exemplars: Paul Bunyan tales; Pecos Bill; American frontier tall tales; Mark Twain's Roughing It.

GENRE 228: Urban Legend
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. "This happened to a friend of a friend"
    2. Ordinary situation established
    3. Strange or threatening element introduced
    4. Twist or revelation
    5. Cautionary moral implied
  Description: Modern folk-narrative presented as having happened to a friend-of-a-friend, typically with horror, ironic, or cautionary twist. Perpetual "almost-true" claim.
  Exemplars: "The Hook"; "The Vanishing Hitchhiker"; "Bloody Mary"; collected by Jan Harold Brunvand.

GENRE 229: Cautionary Tale (general)
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Protagonist tempted toward forbidden choice
    2. Warning given (often ignored)
    3. Choice made
    4. Consequence falls
    5. Moral made explicit
  Description: Narrative whose primary function is warning — depicting a character's poor choice and consequences, so audience may avoid the same fate. Moral instruction through negative example.
  Exemplars: Little Red Riding Hood; Pinocchio; Reefer Madness; Requiem for a Dream.

GENRE 230: Riddle-Narrative
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Riddle posed (often with stakes)
    2. Failed attempts at solution
    3. Insight or trickery
    4. Solution revealed
    5. Consequences of solving (or failing)
  Description: Narrative organized around the posing of a riddle whose solution is the climax. Reader/listener invited to solve along with characters. Intellectual play as the structural pleasure.
  Exemplars: Oedipus and the Sphinx; Bilbo and Gollum; Anglo-Saxon Exeter Book riddles; Turandot.

GENRE 231: Proverb-Narrative
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Situation that will exemplify the proverb
    2. Action illustrating its truth
    3. Outcome
    4. Proverb stated
  Description: Short narrative whose closing line is a proverb or saying that crystallizes the tale's meaning. The story exists to ground and authenticate the proverb. Condensed wisdom validated by example.
  Exemplars: Aesop's fables; West African Akan proverb stories; Yoruba proverb-narratives; Chinese chengyu origin tales.

---

# RELIGIOUS / SPIRITUAL ORAL & WRITTEN FORMS

GENRE 232: Sermon
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Scripture read and contextualized
    2. Exegetical exposition
    3. Theological point developed
    4. Application to listener's life
    5. Closing exhortation or call
  Description: Religious address — typically based on scripture — combining exegesis, moral exhortation, and pastoral application. Interpretive spiritual instruction with explicit application.
  Exemplars: Augustine's sermons; Jonathan Edwards's "Sinners in the Hands of an Angry God"; MLK sermons; Islamic khutbah; Jewish drash.

GENRE 233: Parable
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Ordinary scene established (sower, vineyard, household)
    2. Decisive incident or choice
    3. Outcome that exceeds or inverts expectation
    4. Spiritual application offered or implied
  Description: Short illustrative story in which surface narrative carries spiritual or moral teaching. Distinguished from fable by human characters and religious context. Spiritual truth approached obliquely.
  Exemplars: Parables of Jesus (Good Samaritan, Prodigal Son, Sower); rabbinic mashal; Sufi parables (Rumi, Attar); Buddhist parables.

GENRE 234: Prayer
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Invocation (naming the divine)
    2. Acknowledgment of divine attribute
    3. Confession or thanksgiving
    4. Petition
    5. Closing affirmation ("Amen," "Inshallah")
  Description: Direct address to the divine, usually with structured movement: invocation, acknowledgment, petition, thanksgiving, closing. Speaker-to-divine address across traditions.
  Exemplars: The Lord's Prayer; Islamic Salah; Hindu Sandhyavandanam; Buddhist refuge formulas; secular prayer-shaped meditations.

GENRE 235: Confession narrative
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Speaker's pre-failure self
    2. Sin or failure described
    3. Recognition of wrongness
    4. Confession spoken
    5. Plea for forgiveness or change
  Description: First-person narrative of moral failure and acknowledgment, often within a religious frame seeking forgiveness. Honest accounting before God or community.
  Exemplars: Augustine's Confessions; Tolstoy's A Confession; Rousseau's Confessions; AA / 12-step testimonies.

GENRE 236: Conversion testimony
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Pre-conversion life and its dissatisfactions
    2. Crisis or encounter
    3. Moment of decision or revelation
    4. Visible signs of new life
    5. Closing testimony to others
  Description: First-person narrative of religious conversion. Pre-conversion life, awakening or crisis, decision, transformed post-conversion life. The moment of changed allegiance.
  Exemplars: Paul's Damascus account (Acts 9); John Newton's "Amazing Grace" backstory; Augustine's Confessions; modern evangelical testimony.

GENRE 237: Hagiography (saint's life)
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Birth and early signs of holiness
    2. Early life and calling
    3. Trials and miracles
    4. Spiritual mastery achieved
    5. Death (often with miraculous accompaniment)
    6. Posthumous miracles and devotion
  Description: Biographical narrative of a holy figure — saint, mystic, religious founder — emphasizing miraculous events, exemplary virtue, and posthumous influence. Devotional register.
  Exemplars: Athanasius's Life of Anthony; Sufi tadhkirat al-awliya (Attar); Hindu Bhakti hagiographies; Catholic saints' lives.

GENRE 238: Mystical Vision narrative
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Mystic's prior state
    2. Onset of the experience (often unexpected)
    3. The experience itself (rendered in the limits of language)
    4. Return to ordinary consciousness
    5. Reflection on what was seen and what cannot be said
  Description: First-person account of direct religious experience — vision, ecstasy, union with the divine. Centered on the experience itself, not its conditions.
  Exemplars: Teresa of Ávila's Interior Castle; Julian of Norwich's Revelations; Cloud of Unknowing; Rumi's ecstatic poems; Hildegard of Bingen.

---

# PERFORMANCE & COMMUNAL FORMS

GENRE 239: Eulogy
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Acknowledgment of the gathering and shared loss
    2. Brief life-and-character portrait
    3. Specific stories that embody the deceased
    4. What we lose and what they leave
    5. Closing benediction or farewell
  Description: Speech delivered in honor of a recently-deceased person, typically at the funeral. Holds collective grief while honoring the individual.
  Exemplars: Pericles's funeral oration (Thucydides); famous eulogies in modern public life; family eulogies generally.

GENRE 240: Toast
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Greeting and acknowledgment of the moment
    2. Brief story or reflection on the honoree
    3. The honoree's distinctive quality
    4. Wish for their future
    5. Raising of the glass — "To [honoree]"
  Description: Short speech offered at a celebratory gathering, raising a glass in honor of a person, occasion, or shared value. Brevity, public address, ceremonial close.
  Exemplars: Wedding toasts; retirement toasts; New Year's Eve toasts; many cultural toast traditions.

GENRE 241: Wedding Speech (Best Man / Maid of Honor)
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Greeting and brief self-introduction
    2. How the speaker knows the couple
    3. Funny story or affectionate roast
    4. Sincere observation about the couple's bond
    5. Wish for their future
    6. Toast
  Description: Toast-extension delivered at a wedding by a chosen friend. Mixed tonal register: comedy, sentiment, ceremonial witnessing all in one.
  Exemplars: Wedding speech traditions across cultures; comedy films featuring famous wedding-speech scenes (Four Weddings and a Funeral).

GENRE 242: Roast
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Setup — speaker positioned as "rougher" than usual
    2. Series of mock-insults
    3. Each insult escalates in pointedness
    4. Pivot to genuine affection
    5. Closing acknowledgment of the bond
  Description: Comic ceremonial speech where a person is teased through pointed insults understood as affectionate. The ceremony's structure protects the honoree; affection is underneath the cruelty.
  Exemplars: Friars Club roasts; Comedy Central roasts; Dean Martin's celebrity roasts; modern televised roast formats.

GENRE 243: Acceptance Speech
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Expression of surprise or thanks
    2. Acknowledgment of the awarding body
    3. Specific people thanked
    4. Brief reflection on what the work means
    5. Closing — often a call or a wish
  Description: Speech delivered upon receiving an award or honor. Hybrid: gratitude + brief vision + acknowledgment of others. Engineered for brevity under emotional pressure.
  Exemplars: Oscar acceptance speeches; Nobel Prize lectures; honorary doctorate addresses.

GENRE 244: Commencement Address
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Acknowledgment of the moment
    2. Personal story or memory from the speaker's own life at this stage
    3. Lessons learned (often three)
    4. Direct address to graduates' fears and hopes
    5. Closing benediction or charge
  Description: Speech delivered at graduation ceremony, typically by outside speaker. Engineered for an audience facing transition. Moves from acknowledging the moment to offering wisdom for what comes next.
  Exemplars: David Foster Wallace's "This Is Water"; Steve Jobs's Stanford 2005 address; J.K. Rowling at Harvard; Toni Morrison's commencement addresses.

---

# REALIST FICTION SUB-FORMS

GENRE 245: Social Realism
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Working-class community established
    2. Pressures of economic / political structure shown
    3. Individual struggle within structure
    4. Costs of structure named
    5. Implicit or explicit call for change
  Description: Fiction depicting working-class life, social conditions, and economic structures, typically with reformist intent. Politically-reformist register.
  Exemplars: Steinbeck's The Grapes of Wrath; Sinclair's The Jungle; Richard Wright's Native Son; Sembène's films and novels.

GENRE 246: Naturalism
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Character's inherited and environmental conditions established
    2. Aspirations or efforts to transcend
    3. Forces close in
    4. Inevitable outcome (often degradation or death)
    5. Closing acknowledgment of the deterministic frame
  Description: Fiction grounded in assumption that human behavior is determined by environment, heredity, and circumstance. Quasi-scientific determinism.
  Exemplars: Zola's Rougon-Macquart cycle; Crane's Maggie: A Girl of the Streets; Dreiser's Sister Carrie; Frank Norris's McTeague.

GENRE 247: Kitchen-Sink Realism
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Working-class home in industrial setting
    2. Daily routine rendered
    3. Domestic conflict surfaces
    4. Resolution that changes nothing structural
    5. Continuation of the daily texture
  Description: Mid-20th-c. British genre depicting working-class domestic life with unflinching ordinariness, typically in industrial-Northern settings. Granular texture of working-class home and pub.
  Exemplars: Osborne's Look Back in Anger; Sillitoe's Saturday Night and Sunday Morning; Mike Leigh's films; Ken Loach's films.

GENRE 248: Dirty Realism
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Diminished circumstance established without comment
    2. Small action with large emotional weight
    3. Telling detail accumulates
    4. Quiet turn or non-resolution
    5. Closing image
  Description: Late-20th-c. American minimalist fiction depicting the underside of American life — trailer parks, motels, ordinary failures — with stripped-down prose and unsentimental detail.
  Exemplars: Carver's What We Talk About When We Talk About Love; Richard Ford's Rock Springs; Tobias Wolff; Bobbie Ann Mason.

---

# TRAGEDY FORMS

GENRE 249: Greek Tragedy (proper Aristotelian)
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Prologue sets situation
    2. Parodos (chorus enters)
    3. Episodes of escalating crisis
    4. Stasima (chorus's reflective songs between episodes)
    5. Anagnorisis (recognition) and peripeteia (reversal)
    6. Catastrophe — protagonist's fall
    7. Exodos — final chorus
  Description: Classical Athenian drama as theorized by Aristotle: noble protagonist's reversal through hamartia, evoking pity and fear, achieving catharsis. Fated structure with choral commentary.
  Exemplars: Sophocles' Oedipus Rex; Euripides' Medea; Aeschylus' Oresteia; Sophocles' Antigone.

GENRE 250: Senecan Tragedy
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Exposition with rhetorical declamation
    2. Rising action with ghost or supernatural figure
    3. Climax with rhetorical heightening
    4. Falling action with on-stage violence
    5. Catastrophe with macabre intensity
  Description: Roman tragedy in style of Seneca — five acts, rhetorical heightening, ghosts, on-stage violence, declamation over action. Closet-drama orientation. Heightened rhetoric and macabre intensity.
  Exemplars: Seneca's Thyestes, Medea, Phaedra; influenced Renaissance Revenge Tragedy.

GENRE 251: Revenge Tragedy
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Murder discovered or revealed (often by ghost)
    2. Revenger's commitment to vengeance
    3. Plotting and feigned madness
    4. Initial revenge actions
    5. Moral compromise of the revenger
    6. Climactic bloodbath
    7. All major figures dead
  Description: Renaissance English subgenre. Protagonist seeks revenge for kinsman's murder, undergoes moral degradation, dies with antagonists. Moral consumption through revenge.
  Exemplars: Kyd's The Spanish Tragedy; Shakespeare's Hamlet; Webster's The Duchess of Malfi; Tourneur's The Revenger's Tragedy.

GENRE 252: Domestic Tragedy
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Domestic situation established
    2. Pressure or temptation enters
    3. Family member yields or fails
    4. Consequences fall on the family
    5. Final loss or destruction
  Description: Tragedy set in middle- or working-class domestic life rather than royal settings. Democratization of tragic stature. Ordinary life under tragic pressure.
  Exemplars: Miller's Death of a Salesman; O'Neill's Long Day's Journey Into Night; Williams's A Streetcar Named Desire; Ibsen's A Doll's House.

---

# COMEDY FORMS

GENRE 253: Comedy of Manners
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Social setting and its conventions established
    2. Pretensions and affectations exhibited
    3. Misunderstanding or mismatched expectations
    4. Witty exchanges expose the gap
    5. Affectations punctured (without being destroyed)
  Description: Satirical comedy that mocks the affectations, etiquette, and pretensions of a specific social class. The gap between manners and reality.
  Exemplars: Congreve's The Way of the World; Wilde's The Importance of Being Earnest; Noel Coward's plays; Jane Austen's novels.

GENRE 254: Farce
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Innocent setup
    2. First mistaken identity or unlikely circumstance
    3. Stakes and confusion compound
    4. Chase, hide, mistaken-identity escalation
    5. Climactic moment of total chaos
    6. Resolution — often by exhaustion rather than logic
  Description: Comedy built on improbable situations, mistaken identities, slamming doors, and accelerating chaos. Physical and situational comedy as escalating impossibility.
  Exemplars: Feydeau's farces; Frayn's Noises Off; A Funny Thing Happened on the Way to the Forum; Marx Brothers films.

GENRE 255: Satire
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Target's pretensions established
    2. Exaggeration that exposes
    3. Comparison with absurd parallel
    4. Pretension destroyed by its own logic
    5. Implicit moral revealed
  Description: Comedy with critical intent — exposing folly, hypocrisy, or vice through ridicule. Two main modes: Horatian (gentle) and Juvenalian (savage). Moral-political target.
  Exemplars: Swift's A Modest Proposal; Gulliver's Travels; Catch-22; Dr. Strangelove; Animal Farm.

GENRE 256: Black / Dark Comedy
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Grim or taboo situation established
    2. Treated with comic register
    3. Audience's discomfort and laughter both engaged
    4. Escalation — situation gets worse and funnier
    5. Closing — often without redemptive moral
  Description: Comedy that finds humor in subjects normally treated with seriousness or horror — death, disease, war, atrocity. Uncomfortable laughter; grim reality is funny.
  Exemplars: Dr. Strangelove; MASH; Heller's Catch-22; The Death of Stalin; Fargo; Martin McDonagh's plays.

GENRE 257: Slapstick
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Innocent character in normal situation
    2. First physical mishap
    3. Escalating physical chaos
    4. Climactic large physical event
    5. Relief or recovery
  Description: Physical comedy based on broad, exaggerated, body-based humor — pratfalls, pies in faces, chase sequences. Pure body-driven, language-free comedy.
  Exemplars: Charlie Chaplin's films; Buster Keaton; Three Stooges; Looney Tunes; Mr. Bean.

GENRE 258: Parody
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Target genre or work invoked through stylistic imitation
    2. Conventions exaggerated or made visible
    3. Pleasure of recognition for the audience
    4. Climactic over-fulfillment of the convention
    5. Closing wink
  Description: Comedy that imitates the style and conventions of a specific work or genre to comic effect. The formal target is what's being mocked.
  Exemplars: Mel Brooks's Young Frankenstein, Spaceballs; Airplane!; This Is Spinal Tap; Galaxy Quest; Cervantes's Don Quixote.

---

# MUSICAL & PERFORMANCE FORMS

GENRE 259: Opera narrative
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Overture sets emotional palette
    2. Act 1 — situation, principals introduced through arias
    3. Conflict and intrigue develop
    4. Act 2-3 — crisis, betrayal, climactic arias
    5. Final act — death, reunion, or transformation
    6. Closing chorus
  Description: Sung-through dramatic narrative combining music, poetry, and theater. 3-4 acts with arias for character interiority, ensembles for conflict. Heightened emotion sustainable only through music.
  Exemplars: Verdi's La Traviata; Puccini's La Bohème; Mozart's Don Giovanni; Wagner's Ring Cycle; Adams's Nixon in China.

GENRE 260: Broadway Musical
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. "I Want" song — protagonist's desire stated
    2. Inciting event in spoken scene
    3. Series of book scenes alternating with songs
    4. Act 1 close — usually a high-energy ensemble
    5. Act 2 — complications and 11 o'clock number
    6. Resolution and finale
  Description: American musical-theater form combining book (spoken scenes), songs, and dance. English vernacular and integrated book-songs structure.
  Exemplars: West Side Story; Hamilton; Les Misérables; Chicago; Sweeney Todd; Wicked.

GENRE 261: Ballet narrative
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Setting established through ensemble
    2. Principal characters introduced through pas de deux
    3. Complication or magical event
    4. Variation pieces explore character
    5. Climactic confrontation or transformation
    6. Resolution through grand ensemble
  Description: Narrative dance work, typically without text, communicating story through choreography, music, costume, and staging. Body-narrative — story through bodies in motion.
  Exemplars: Tchaikovsky's Swan Lake, The Nutcracker, Sleeping Beauty; Stravinsky's The Rite of Spring; Giselle.

---

# ADDITIONAL MARKETING

GENRE 262: Testimonial Ad
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Customer introduced
    2. Pre-product problem
    3. Discovery of product
    4. Use
    5. Outcome
    6. Closing recommendation
  Description: Advertising in which a real (or plausible) customer narrates their experience with the product. Peer credibility rather than authority endorsement.
  Exemplars: Most consumer-product advertising; "real people, not actors" campaigns; modern Instagram ad creator content.

GENRE 263: Comparison Ad
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Competitor named or implied
    2. Side-by-side comparison
    3. Key advantage demonstrated
    4. Conclusion in product's favor
  Description: Advertising that explicitly compares the product to a competitor's, highlighting advantages. Named adversarial structure.
  Exemplars: Apple's "Mac vs. PC" campaign; Pepsi Challenge; Tesla's silent-comparison-with-gas-cars marketing.

GENRE 264: Lifestyle Ad
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Aspirational scene established
    2. Subject living the lifestyle
    3. Product as natural part of the scene
    4. Implication: this is who you could be
  Description: Advertising that sells the product through association with an aspirational way of life rather than functional features. Identity-coded value.
  Exemplars: Ralph Lauren campaigns; Apple's "Shot on iPhone"; Patagonia's outdoor narratives; luxury-goods advertising.

GENRE 265: Before / After Ad
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Before state — pain, lack, problem
    2. Discovery of product
    3. Use
    4. After state — pleasure, fulfillment, solution
    5. Call to action
  Description: Advertising that shows the subject's state before using the product and after, with the product as transformative agent. Direct, conversion-focused.
  Exemplars: Weight-loss ads; home-renovation shows; before-and-after marketing across industries.

GENRE 266: Product Launch Story
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Problem in the world
    2. Insight that produced solution
    3. Demonstration of product
    4. Stories of early use
    5. Implications for the future
    6. Call to acquire
  Description: Narrative announcing a new product, woven around the problem the product solves, the insight that produced it, and the future it makes possible.
  Exemplars: Apple keynote launches (Steve Jobs's iPhone, 2007); Tesla unveilings; SaaS-product launch posts.

---

# ADDITIONAL POLITICAL

GENRE 267: Filibuster narrative
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Procedural opening
    2. Substantive content (often reading from prepared materials)
    3. Personal anecdote
    4. Address to fellow members
    5. Continued speaking
    6. Eventual yielding or vote
  Description: Extended speech delivered to delay legislative action — the act of speaking is itself the political move. Hybrid: substantive content + procedural function.
  Exemplars: Strom Thurmond's 24-hour filibuster (1957); Wendy Davis's Texas filibuster (2013); Bernie Sanders 2010; Mr. Smith Goes to Washington.

GENRE 268: Town Hall Address
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Greeting and acknowledgment of community
    2. Brief remarks
    3. Open Q&A
    4. Direct response to specific questions
    5. Closing thanks
  Description: Politician's direct engagement with constituents, structured as remarks followed by Q&A. Semi-improvised middle and accountability-under-pressure tenor.
  Exemplars: Modern American town halls; British MP constituency surgeries; presidential town hall debates.

GENRE 269: Debate format
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Opening statements
    2. Question 1 with each side's response
    3. Rebuttals
    4. Successive questions
    5. Closing statements
  Description: Structured public confrontation between candidates or positions, governed by formal rules of time, response, and rebuttal. Contest visible to voters.
  Exemplars: Lincoln-Douglas debates (1858); Kennedy-Nixon (1960); modern presidential debates; British Question Time.

---

# RELIGIOUS & SPIRITUAL NARRATIVE (long-form)

GENRE 270: Pilgrimage Memoir
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Decision to undertake
    2. Departure
    3. Stages of the journey, each with a lesson
    4. Crisis or doubt
    5. Arrival at the sacred destination
    6. Encounter with the holy
    7. Return home, transformed
  Description: First-person narrative of a religious or spiritual pilgrimage — Mecca, Jerusalem, Camino, Varanasi, Mount Kailash. The journey is interior as well as exterior.
  Exemplars: Bashō's Oku no Hosomichi; Cheryl Strayed's Wild (secular); The Way of a Pilgrim; Robert Macfarlane's The Old Ways.

GENRE 271: Spiritual Autobiography
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Childhood spiritual context
    2. Awakening or first calling
    3. Trials of faith
    4. Crisis or dark night
    5. Mature faith
    6. Reflections on lifelong learning
  Description: Long-form first-person account of a life's spiritual development. Distinct from secular autobiography by emphasis on the soul's journey.
  Exemplars: Augustine's Confessions; Teresa of Ávila's Life; Merton's The Seven Storey Mountain; Yogananda's The Autobiography of a Yogi; C.S. Lewis's Surprised by Joy.

GENRE 272: Allegorical Religious Narrative
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Allegorical world established
    2. Protagonist sets out on figural journey
    3. Each encounter represents a spiritual challenge
    4. Trials map onto soul-stages
    5. Arrival at allegorical destination
    6. Allegorical key revealed (sometimes)
  Description: Narrative whose surface story figures a deeper spiritual journey — characters, places, and events stand for spiritual states or lessons. Spiritual content encountered through story rather than argument.
  Exemplars: Bunyan's The Pilgrim's Progress; Everyman; Spenser's The Faerie Queene; Hesse's Siddhartha; Coelho's The Alchemist.

---

# EDUCATIONAL GENRES

GENRE 273: Lecture
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Hook
    2. Learning objectives
    3. Background
    4. Main content delivered in 2-4 sub-sections
    5. Examples and applications
    6. Synthesis
    7. Q&A
  Description: Structured oral presentation of a topic by an expert to learners, typically 30-60 minutes. Pedagogical (not peer-review) audience.
  Exemplars: University lectures; MOOC video lectures; Great Courses productions; classical vorlesungen.

GENRE 274: Tutorial / How-To Lesson
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Skill stated
    2. Why it matters
    3. Materials needed
    4. Steps demonstrated
    5. Common mistakes flagged
    6. Practice prompt
  Description: Step-by-step instructional content designed to teach a specific skill or process. Operational aim: learner can DO something at the end.
  Exemplars: Instructional videos; cooking shows; technical tutorials; coding bootcamp lessons.

GENRE 275: Textbook Chapter
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Learning objectives
    2. Vocabulary
    3. Conceptual exposition
    4. Worked examples
    5. Application problems
    6. Summary
    7. Review questions
    8. Further reading
  Description: Structured educational chapter combining exposition, examples, and review questions. Engineered for systematic curriculum coverage.
  Exemplars: University-level textbooks; K-12 textbooks; standard educational publishing.

---

GENRE 276: TED Talk format
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Hook (often a personal story)
    2. Big idea introduced
    3. Evidence and examples
    4. Implications
    5. Closing call or vision
    6. A single image to remember
  Description: Short (5-18 minute) talk format engineered for memorability and viral sharing. Combines personal story, big idea, and call to action. One big idea worth spreading.
  Exemplars: Brené Brown's "Power of Vulnerability"; Sir Ken Robinson's "Do Schools Kill Creativity?"; Hans Rosling; Simon Sinek's "Start With Why."

GENRE 277: Khan Academy / Video Tutorial
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Concept stated
    2. Why it matters
    3. Worked example with narration
    4. Variation or related example
    5. Common mistakes flagged
    6. Practice prompt
  Description: Short, screen-captured video lesson explaining a single concept with examples — common in modern online education. Specific compressed-video format.
  Exemplars: Khan Academy videos; 3Blue1Brown's math videos; Crash Course; Veritasium; Numberphile.

GENRE 278: MOOC Module
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Module learning objectives
    2. Video lectures
