GENRE 694: Popular Science Book
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Scientific question or field framed accessibly
    2. Historical context of the science
    3. Key concepts explained with analogies
    4. Researchers and their stories
    5. Current state of understanding
    6. Implications for the reader
  Description: Long-form book translating scientific findings or fields for general audiences. Bridging-academic-and-popular register.
  Exemplars: Stephen Hawking's A Brief History of Time; Richard Dawkins's The Selfish Gene; Mary Roach's books; Bill Bryson's A Short History of Nearly Everything; Carl Sagan.

GENRE 695: Science Magazine Feature
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Hook (often human story or vivid scene)
    2. Science introduced through the hook
    3. Researchers profiled
    4. Findings explained
    5. Implications and controversies
    6. Closing scene or reflection
  Description: Long-form magazine article on scientific topic. Length and journalistic-magazine register distinct from popular-science book.
  Exemplars: Wired features; Atlantic science features; Quanta Magazine; New Yorker science pieces.

GENRE 696: Citizen Science Narrative
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Project introduced
    2. Volunteers' background
    3. Methodology accessible to amateurs
    4. Specific contributions
    5. Findings produced
    6. Implications for science and participants
  Description: Narrative of non-scientist volunteers contributing to scientific research — biodiversity counts, astronomical observation, archaeological discovery.
  Exemplars: eBird-related narratives; iNaturalist project stories; SETI@home accounts; Galaxy Zoo discoveries.

GENRE 697: Climate Narrative / Eco-Reportage
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Specific climate phenomenon or place
    2. Affected people
    3. Science explained
    4. Policy and politics
    5. What's being done (or not)
    6. Implications for the future
  Description: Long-form narrative reporting on climate change — its causes, effects, responses. Non-fiction commitment distinct from cli-fi.
  Exemplars: Elizabeth Kolbert's The Sixth Extinction; David Wallace-Wells's The Uninhabitable Earth; Naomi Klein's This Changes Everything; Bill McKibben's writings.

GENRE 698: Environmental Writing
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Specific place or species
    2. History of human relationship with it
    3. Threats faced
    4. Conservation work
    5. Political and ethical stakes
    6. Call to engagement
  Description: Long-form writing on environmental themes — wilderness, conservation, ecological loss. Specifically political-environmental register.
  Exemplars: Aldo Leopold's A Sand County Almanac; Edward Abbey's Desert Solitaire; Wendell Berry's essays; Robin Wall Kimmerer's Braiding Sweetgrass.

GENRE 699: Conservation Narrative
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Threatened species or ecosystem
    2. Conservation effort begins
    3. Methods and challenges
    4. Specific recovery or loss events
    5. Climactic moment
    6. Ongoing or successful conservation
  Description: Narrative of conservation effort — saving species, protecting land, restoring ecosystem. Conservation-success-or-struggle register.
  Exemplars: American Buffalo (Burns documentary); Jane Goodall's chimp work; The Whale and the Reactor (Winner); California condor recovery narratives.

GENRE 700: Deep-Time / Geological Narrative
  Tradition: Universal
  Laws (beats):
    1. Present-day landscape or phenomenon
    2. Geological/temporal frame stretched
    3. Deep history traced
    4. Forces and timescales rendered
    5. Climactic perspective shift
    6. Return to present, transformed
  Description: Writing exploring deep geological or cosmic time — how landscapes formed, how species evolved, how the universe will end. Time-scale-shifting register.
  Exemplars: John McPhee's Annals of the Former World; Robert Macfarlane's Underland; Annie Dillard's deep-time passages.

---

# NGC ORIGINAL GENRES (batch 2 — communicative shapes)

GENRE 701: The Quiet Pivot
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Old way held
    2. Inner shift recognized
    3. Quiet new behavior, undeclared
  Description: A protagonist undergoes a small but irreversible internal change without external announcement. The change is real but unaccompanied by ceremony or public statement. Use when the meaningful turn happens privately.
  Exemplars: Many literary-fiction interior turns; quiet-resignation arcs; mid-life recalibration essays.

GENRE 702: Recognition Without Words
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Tension or distance held between two
    2. Meeting of eyes or shared moment
    3. Bond confirmed without speech
  Description: Two characters arrive at understanding without speaking. The change is mutual and silent — the recognition itself is the structural payoff.
  Exemplars: Many film moments (the long look); literary-fiction reconciliation scenes; certain estrangement-end moments.

GENRE 703: Inheritance Refused
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Inheritance offered (literal, metaphorical, generational)
    2. Recognition of what taking it would cost
    3. Polite refusal kept
  Description: Receiving a legacy — money, authority, family role, ideological mantle — and choosing not to take it. Distinct from rejection (hot) by being quiet and respectful.
  Exemplars: Family-business successor narratives; ideological-second-generation refusals; literary "leaving the firm" arcs.

GENRE 704: The Return-to-Origin
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Departure long ago
    2. Long absence and accumulated change
    3. Return seeing what once was, differently
  Description: A protagonist comes back to where they began — physically, relationally, vocationally — and sees it transformed by their own change. Use when the genre's content is the gap between memory and present perception.
  Exemplars: Hometown-return memoirs; reunion scenes in literary fiction; Annie Dillard's An American Childhood register.

GENRE 705: Found Through Loss
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Loss occurs
    2. Search begins for unrelated thing
    3. Recognition that the loss made the finding possible
  Description: What was sought is gained only through losing something else. The structural insight is the inversion: the gain was hidden inside the loss.
  Exemplars: Recovery memoirs where bottom enables sobriety; vocational-reinvention essays; certain grief-memoir resolutions.

GENRE 706: Permission Granted
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Held desire (long-suppressed)
    2. External or internal sanction received
    3. Action freed
  Description: Receiving — from elder, mentor, partner, or self — the right to do what one already wanted. The genre's payoff is the *release* the permission unlocks.
  Exemplars: Coming-of-age threshold scenes; retirement-permission narratives; mid-life "you can stop now" arcs.

GENRE 707: The Naming
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Unnamed thing carried (feeling, condition, dynamic)
    2. Name uttered for the first time
    3. World changed by the naming
  Description: Calling something by its true name for the first time. The act of naming is the structural pivot — what was diffuse becomes addressable.
  Exemplars: Diagnosis moments; "I'm queer" / "I'm an alcoholic" / "this is abuse" identification arcs; many therapeutic breakthroughs.

GENRE 708: Companion-in-the-Dark
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Crisis enters another's life
    2. Sustained witness without intervention
    3. Crisis passes; bond deepened
  Description: Sustained presence through another's worst time without trying to fix. The genre's content is loyalty-as-witness rather than loyalty-as-action.
  Exemplars: Many caregiver-narrative quiet sequences; deep-friendship grief arcs; therapeutic-presence narratives.

GENRE 709: Unannounced Mastery
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Underestimation
    2. Quiet doing of the difficult thing
    3. Recognition (often elsewhere, or never directly)
  Description: Quiet competence revealed without performance. The protagonist does the hard thing without announcement; recognition (if it comes) is incidental.
  Exemplars: Workplace quiet-excellence narratives; behind-the-scenes hero stories; many older-craftsman profiles.

GENRE 710: The Belated Apology
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Old wound carried for years
    2. Late acknowledgment delivered
    3. Whatever-comes-next, including refusal
  Description: Apology delivered years after the wound. The genre's content is what late accountability is and isn't — and the recipient's right to whatever response.
  Exemplars: Reconciliation memoirs; long-deferred-letter narratives; modern accountability essays.

GENRE 711: Tender Surrender
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Held thing (relationship, role, dream)
    2. Soft release, not rupture
    3. Continuation onward, gentler
  Description: Letting something go without struggle, with affection. Distinct from defeat (hot) and from refusal (firm) — the release is *kind*.
  Exemplars: End-of-relationship scenes that are not breakups; closing-of-a-business arcs; child-leaves-home narratives in their soft form.

GENRE 712: Borrowed Light
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Need for strength one does not yet have
    2. Borrowed strength from another's wisdom or example
    3. Eventual self-light
  Description: Living temporarily inside another's wisdom or grace until one's own arrives. The structural insight is that borrowed strength can become real.
  Exemplars: Mentorship-period memoirs; therapy-narrative sequences; "she believed in me until I could" arcs.

GENRE 713: The Hand Held Out
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Witness of need
    2. Offer made (without forcing)
    3. Whatever response, including no response
  Description: Offering help that may or may not be accepted. The genre's payoff is the *offer itself* — the value is in extending the hand, not in the result.
  Exemplars: Outreach-to-estranged-family essays; reaching-back-to-former-self narratives; many small kindness anecdotes.

GENRE 714: Unfinished Business
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Unresolved thing carried
    2. Recognition it will not resolve
    3. Continuing-with the unresolved
  Description: Acknowledging that some things will never close. Distinct from closure (resolution) and from denial (avoidance) — the protagonist learns to *live alongside* the unfinished.
  Exemplars: Late-grief memoirs; certain estrangement narratives; long-illness reflective essays.

GENRE 715: The Witnessing Circle
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Crisis or wound brought to community
    2. Group bears witness without trying to fix
    3. Something restored by the act of witnessing
  Description: Communal acknowledgment that creates change without action. The collective presence is itself the intervention. Distinct from group therapy (which intervenes) by emphasizing pure witness.
  Exemplars: Truth and Reconciliation Commission narratives; Quaker meeting moments; some recovery-meeting structures; certain grief-circle traditions.

---

# CULTURAL / REGIONAL ADDITIONS

GENRE 716: Kathāsaritsāgara frame-tale (कथासरित्सागर)
  Tradition: Indian
  Laws (beats):
    1. Outer frame story established (often a king, sage, or supernatural figure)
    2. First nested tale begins
    3. Tales nest inside tales (often three or four deep)
    4. Each tale illustrates a moral or wisdom
    5. Resolution returns through nested layers to the frame
    6. Frame's question answered through accumulation
  Description: 11th-century Sanskrit "Ocean of Stories" frame-tale collection by Somadeva. Foundational to Indian frame-narrative tradition; influenced Pañchatantra and Arabian Nights.
  Exemplars: Somadeva's Kathāsaritsāgara; Bṛhatkathā tradition; Vetāla Pañcaviṃśati (25 tales of the vampire).

GENRE 717: Sufi Qissa narrative (قصة)
  Tradition: Persian-Arabic
  Laws (beats):
    1. Seeker introduced
    2. Spiritual master encountered
    3. Trial or paradox presented
    4. Seeker's struggle
    5. Insight or surrender
    6. New state of being
  Description: Sufi spiritual tale of seeker-and-master, paradox-and-insight. Distinct from generic Sufi parable by longer, more elaborated narrative arc.
  Exemplars: Tales in Attar's Conference of the Birds; Rumi's narrative passages in Masnavi; classical qissa collections.

GENRE 718: Hikaya (Arabic short tale, حكاية)
  Tradition: Persian-Arabic
  Laws (beats):
    1. Setting and characters quickly established
    2. Surprising event or encounter
    3. Brief twist or revelation
    4. Quick close with implied lesson
  Description: Short Arabic tale form — typically a single anecdote with a moral or surprising turn. Distinct from longer dastan or maqama by compression.
  Exemplars: Tales within Thousand and One Nights (the shorter ones); al-Tanukhi's Nishwar al-Muhadara anecdotes; Ibn al-Jawzi's collections.

GENRE 719: Aboriginal Dreamtime narrative
  Tradition: Indigenous-Americas
  Laws (beats):
    1. Ancestral being named
    2. Country (specific land) established
    3. Ancestor's journey or action
    4. Land features formed by the action
    5. Ancestor's transformation or settlement
    6. Ongoing relationship between living people and the Dreaming
  Description: Australian Aboriginal narrative of the Dreaming (Tjukurrpa, Alcheringa, etc.) — ancestral beings shape Country through their journeys; living people continue these stories. Sacred and place-specific.
  Exemplars: Yolngu Songlines; Pitjantjatjara Tjukurrpa narratives; Bruce Chatwin's Songlines (external account); modern Indigenous-authored versions.
  Notes: Sacred to specific peoples; this entry documents the structural form, not protected ritual content.

GENRE 720: Maori whakapapa narrative
  Tradition: Indigenous-Americas
  Laws (beats):
    1. Origin (Te Kore, Te Pō, Te Ao Mārama)
    2. Ancestral lineage traced from primordial parents
    3. Generation-by-generation descent
    4. Connection to land (whenua), tribe (iwi), and present speaker
    5. Closing acknowledgment of present standing
  Description: Maori (New Zealand) genealogical narrative form — recitation of descent from primordial origins through ancestors to the speaker, establishing identity and standing.
  Exemplars: Maori whakapapa recitations; oral tradition documented in Buck's The Coming of the Maori; modern Maori-authored sources.

GENRE 721: Polynesian moʻolelo (voyage narrative)
  Tradition: Indigenous-Americas
  Laws (beats):
    1. Departure from home island
    2. Reading of stars, currents, and birds
    3. Long voyage with crisis points
    4. Sighting of new island
    5. Naming and establishing place
    6. Genealogical connection from voyager to present
  Description: Polynesian wayfinding-and-settlement narrative — voyaging across vast Pacific distances, claiming and naming islands, establishing lineages.
  Exemplars: Hawaiian moʻolelo of Hawaiʻi-loa; Cook Islands voyage narratives; Marquesan and Tahitian wayfinding traditions; modern Hokuleʻa documentation.

GENRE 722: Kebra Nagast narrative (Ethiopian)
  Tradition: West-African
  Laws (beats):
    1. Ancient kingdom established
    2. Encounter between Solomon and Queen of Sheba
    3. Birth of Menelik I
    4. Menelik's journey to Jerusalem
    5. Return with the Ark of the Covenant
    6. Founding of the Solomonic dynasty in Ethiopia
  Description: Ethiopian "Glory of the Kings" — foundational narrative of the Solomonic dynasty, integrating biblical origin with Ethiopian sovereignty and the Ark.
  Exemplars: Kebra Nagast text (14th-c. compilation, older traditions); modern Ethiopian Orthodox liturgical recitations.

GENRE 723: Swahili utenzi epic
  Tradition: West-African
  Laws (beats):
    1. Invocation of God and Prophet
    2. Hero's noble lineage introduced
    3. Battle or quest narrative
    4. Trials and supernatural encounters
    5. Climactic battle
    6. Hero's piety and conclusion
  Description: Swahili long narrative poem, typically Islamic-heroic in subject. Composed in quatrains; often celebrating prophetic figures, battles, or moral heroes.
  Exemplars: Utenzi wa Tambuka (Epic of Heraclius); Utenzi wa Liyongo; Islamic Swahili poetic tradition (East African coast, 17th c. onward).

GENRE 724: Akan Anansesem (formal Anansi tales)
  Tradition: West-African
  Laws (beats):
    1. Opening formula ("My story which I am about to tell, let it come, let it go")
    2. Anansi's situation and desire
    3. Anansi's scheme
    4. Action against larger figure (sky-god, lion, elephant)
    5. Consequence (often etiological)
    6. Closing formula
  Description: Formal Akan storytelling tradition with stock opening and closing formulae. Distinct from generic Anansi cycle by its specific performative-frame conventions.
  Exemplars: Akan oral storytelling tradition (Ghana, Ivory Coast); R.S. Rattray's Akan-Ashanti Folk-Tales (1930); modern Asante storytelling.

GENRE 725: Igbo Ifo folk narrative
  Tradition: West-African
  Laws (beats):
    1. Opening call-and-response between teller and audience
    2. Setting in mythic past
    3. Animal or human character with desire or transgression
    4. Action and consequence
    5. Moral revealed
    6. Closing formula
  Description: Igbo (Nigeria) folk narrative form, typically performed orally with audience participation. Often features tortoise (Mbe) as trickster; etiological or moral.
  Exemplars: Tortoise stories collected in Achebe's Things Fall Apart and No Longer at Ease; modern Igbo folkloric collections; Nwapa's Idu and Efuru.

GENRE 726: Quechua / Andean origin narrative
  Tradition: Indigenous-Americas
  Laws (beats):
    1. Pre-Inca world or chaos
    2. Manco Cápac and Mama Ocllo emerge from Lake Titicaca
    3. Journey to find sacred place
    4. Founding of Cuzco
    5. Establishment of Inca order
    6. Connection to present land and people
  Description: Andean origin narrative — Inca founding myth and broader Quechua cosmology. Specific connection to landscape (apus, huacas) and sacred geography.
  Exemplars: Inca Garcilaso de la Vega's Royal Commentaries; Felipe Guamán Poma de Ayala's Nueva crónica; modern Quechua oral tradition.

GENRE 727: Mapuche oral narrative (Epeu)
  Tradition: Indigenous-Americas
  Laws (beats):
    1. Mapuche cosmology established (four worlds, ancestors)
    2. Mythic time situation
    3. Beings (people, spirits, animals) interact
    4. Transformation or test
    5. Lesson or origin established
    6. Connection to present Mapuche land (Wallmapu)
  Description: Mapuche (Chile/Argentina) oral narrative form. Epeu (story) and ngütram (historical narrative) are distinct sub-forms.
  Exemplars: Mapuche oral tradition documented by Manuel Mansilla and others; modern Mapudungun-language collections.

GENRE 728: Aztec codex narrative
  Tradition: Indigenous-Americas
  Laws (beats):
    1. Pre-Hispanic Mesoamerican cosmology established
    2. Specific gods or heroes acting
    3. Five-Sun cosmology (current Sun's place)
    4. Historical migration or founding event
    5. Tenochtitlán's establishment (specific to Mexica)
    6. Lineage continuing to present
  Description: Aztec/Mexica historical-mythological narrative form, originally pictorial-textual codices. Records origin, migration, dynastic succession, and prophecy.
  Exemplars: Codex Boturini (Tira de la Peregrinación); Florentine Codex (Sahagún); Codex Mendoza; modern Mexica-authored reconstructions.

GENRE 729: Russian skazka (folk tale)
  Tradition: Celtic-Norse
  Laws (beats):
    1. Opening "In a certain kingdom, in a certain land..."
    2. Hero (often Ivan-the-Fool) introduced
    3. Quest or task assigned
    4. Encounter with magical helper (Baba Yaga, firebird)
    5. Trials and resolution
    6. "And I was there, and drank mead and wine..." closing
  Description: Russian folk-tale tradition — distinct from broader European folk-tale by specific stock figures (Baba Yaga, Koshchei the Deathless, Ivan-Tsarevich) and characteristic openings/closings.
  Exemplars: Afanasyev's Russian Folk-Tales; Pushkin's verse adaptations; Bilibin illustrations; modern Russian-language collections.
  Notes: Categorized as Celtic-Norse for European-Slavic placement; could be its own tradition in future expansion.

GENRE 730: Persian Shāhnāmeh / Book of Kings
  Tradition: Persian-Arabic
  Laws (beats):
    1. Cosmic origin and first king
    2. Pishdadian dynasty (mythic kings)
    3. Kayanian dynasty (legendary heroes — Rostam, Sohrab)
    4. Heroic battles and tragedies
    5. Sasanian dynasty (historical kings)
    6. Arab conquest and ending
  Description: Ferdowsi's 10th-c. Persian national epic — 50,000 couplets covering kings and heroes from creation to Sasanian fall. Foundation of Persian literary identity.
  Exemplars: Ferdowsi's Shāhnāmeh; Dick Davis's English translation; modern Persian editions and recitations.

GENRE 731: Tibetan Gesar epic
  Tradition: Indigenous-Americas
  Laws (beats):
    1. Heaven's appointment of hero
    2. Gesar's miraculous birth
    3. Trials in childhood
    4. Becoming king of Ling
    5. Series of campaigns against demonic forces
    6. Return to heaven
  Description: Central Asian / Tibetan / Mongolian oral epic — longest in the world, with millions of lines across versions. UNESCO Intangible Cultural Heritage.
  Exemplars: Tibetan Gesar tradition; Mongolian Geser; Buryat versions; living oral epic with master-singers (drungba).
  Notes: Categorized as Indigenous-Americas placeholder for Inner Asian tradition; could be its own category in future.

GENRE 732: Mongolian heroic epic (tuuli)
  Tradition: Indigenous-Americas
  Laws (beats):
    1. Hero's noble birth
    2. Departure on quest or campaign
    3. Battles with monsters or rival khans
    4. Acquisition of bride or treasure
    5. Return triumph
    6. Establishment of legacy
  Description: Mongolian long narrative poem (tuuli, ülger) performed by epic-singers (tuulchin) with khoomei (throat-singing) and morin khuur (horse-head fiddle).
  Exemplars: Jangar epic; Geser cycle (Mongolian variant); Khalkha and Oirat oral traditions; modern UNESCO documentation.

GENRE 733: Filipino Hudhud / Ifugao epic
  Tradition: Indigenous-Americas
  Laws (beats):
    1. Hero's family and rice-terrace setting
    2. Departure for adventure or quest
    3. Encounter with rival or supernatural
    4. Test of strength or wit
    5. Marriage or treasure-acquisition
    6. Return with honor
  Description: Ifugao (Philippines) chanted oral epic, performed during rice-harvest and ritual occasions. UNESCO Intangible Cultural Heritage.
  Exemplars: Hudhud chants of the Ifugao (Cordillera); Manuel Dulawan's collections; modern Ifugao cultural preservation.

GENRE 734: Yiddish folk narrative
  Tradition: Celtic-Norse
  Laws (beats):
    1. Shtetl setting established
    2. Ordinary Jewish protagonist (often a fool, scholar, or wise woman)
    3. Encounter with otherness (gentile, demon, miracle)
    4. Wit, faith, or comedy resolves
    5. Lesson rooted in Jewish ethical-religious tradition
    6. Closing often poignant or comic
  Description: Yiddish folk-tale tradition (Eastern European Jewish) — shtetl life, golems, dybbuks, Hasidic tales, Chelm fool stories. Specific cultural-religious context.
  Exemplars: I.B. Singer's stories; tales of Chelm; Chassidic rabbinic narratives; Hershele Ostropoler; The Dybbuk (S. An-sky).
  Notes: Categorized under Celtic-Norse for European placement; deserves own tradition in future expansion.

GENRE 735: Romani oral narrative
  Tradition: Celtic-Norse
  Laws (beats):
    1. Wandering protagonist or family
    2. Encounter with settled society (gadjo)
    3. Trickery, revelation, or lesson
    4. Outcome that affirms Romani identity or wisdom
    5. Continuation onward
  Description: Romani folk-narrative tradition — wandering, outsider-perspective, often featuring trickster motifs and Romani moral framing distinct from settled European traditions.
  Exemplars: Romani oral collections by Ian Hancock and others; modern Romani-authored literature; tales documented across European Romani communities.

---

# NGC ORIGINAL GENRES — Communicative 3-Beat Shapes (Phase 7 seeds)

> 3-beat shapes for short-form, conversational, everyday communication. These are the most basic communicative narratives — designed for tweets, captions, replies, short emails, brand lines, conversational responses. Each is exactly 3 beats: Setup → Shift → Resolution.

GENRE 736: Apology (compressed)
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Acknowledgment of specific wrong
    2. Acceptance of impact
    3. Concrete commitment to change
  Description: 3-beat compressed apology — for short conversational, social-media, or direct-message contexts. Distinct from formal apology letter (612) by compression.
  Exemplars: Effective public apology tweets; brief in-conversation apologies; therapeutic accountability sentences.

GENRE 737: Brag (compressed)
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Setup of stakes or context
    2. Specific deed accomplished
    3. Frame that lets the recipient celebrate without awkwardness
  Description: 3-beat self-celebration shape — sharing accomplishment in a way that invites others in rather than alienates. Distinct from boast by tonal awareness.
  Exemplars: Effective LinkedIn announcement posts; casual-text celebrations; "humble brag" done well.

GENRE 738: Reveal (compressed)
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Apparent surface
    2. Crack — first sign of something underneath
    3. True picture emerges
  Description: 3-beat shape for disclosing a hidden truth — about a person, situation, or product. Universal communicative arc for unmasking.
  Exemplars: Investigative-tweet threads; product-flaw disclosures; personal-disclosure micro-essays.

GENRE 739: Lesson Learned (compressed)
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Wrong belief or expectation held
    2. Painful event or experience
    3. New belief or insight
  Description: 3-beat shape for the most basic teaching narrative — what I used to think, what happened, what I think now. Common in advice content, personal essays, professional reflections.
  Exemplars: "What I learned from..." posts; advice-column structure; mentoring micro-stories.

GENRE 740: Recommendation (compressed)
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Need or problem identified
    2. Find — the recommended thing encountered
    3. Resolve — why it solves the problem
  Description: 3-beat shape for recommending a product, book, person, place. Most basic structure for word-of-mouth.
  Exemplars: Product reviews; book recommendations; "you should try..." messages.

GENRE 741: Complaint (compressed)
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Expectation set
    2. Failure to meet it
    3. Demand or call for redress
  Description: 3-beat shape for grievance — to a company, institution, or person. Specifies what was promised, what happened, what's owed.
  Exemplars: Customer complaints; HR grievances; political letters of complaint; angry tweets that work.

GENRE 742: Gratitude (compressed)
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Receipt of gift or kindness
    2. Recognition of what it cost the giver or what it meant
    3. Acknowledgment that creates ongoing relationship
  Description: 3-beat shape for thanks — distinct from formal thank-you by being conversational and immediate. The structural payoff is the relationship the gratitude creates.
  Exemplars: Effective thank-you texts; team appreciation messages; intimate gratitude expressions.

GENRE 743: Confession (compressed)
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Hidden thing carried
    2. Cost of carrying it named
    3. Disclosure delivered
  Description: 3-beat shape for telling someone something hidden — affair, lie, mistake, identity. Distinct from formal confession by conversational immediacy.
  Exemplars: Coming-out conversations; "I have to tell you something..." disclosures; therapeutic confessions.

GENRE 744: Hot Take (compressed)
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Common belief named
    2. Counter-claim asserted
    3. Why it matters or what changes
  Description: 3-beat shape for the contrarian opinion — most common rhetorical move on social media, in op-eds, in conversations. Provocation done well.
  Exemplars: Effective Twitter hot-take threads; opinion-essay leads; "actually..." conversations done thoughtfully.

GENRE 745: Pivot (compressed)
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Was — old direction, identity, or commitment
    2. Why-change — reason for the turn
    3. Now — current direction or commitment
  Description: 3-beat shape for announcing a change — career, project, position, identity. Distinct from career pivot narrative by extreme compression.
  Exemplars: "I'm leaving X to do Y" announcements; brand pivot communications; personal transition posts.

GENRE 746: Realization (compressed)
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Status quo assumed
    2. Trigger — moment that disturbed it
    3. Insight — new understanding
  Description: 3-beat shape for the small epiphany — the conversational "I just realized..." moment. Most basic structure for sharing personal revelation.
  Exemplars: Casual "I just realized..." texts; therapy-breakthrough recountings; reflective journal entries.

GENRE 747: Goodbye (compressed)
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Bond named
    2. End acknowledged
    3. What is carried forward
  Description: 3-beat shape for parting — leaving a job, ending a friendship, closing a chapter. Brief, dignified, and forward-tilted.
  Exemplars: Farewell emails; closing speeches; intimate goodbyes; retirement announcements.

GENRE 748: Greeting (compressed)
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Acknowledgment of the other
    2. Bridge — shared context or moment
    3. Open — invitation to engage further
  Description: 3-beat shape for opening conversation, message, or relationship. Most common structure for first-contact communications.
  Exemplars: Effective cold introductions; networking openers; rekindling-old-contact messages.

GENRE 749: Sales Pitch (compressed)
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Pain — problem the customer has
    2. Promise — what we deliver
    3. Path — how to get it
  Description: 3-beat shape for selling — product, service, idea. Most basic conversion structure.
  Exemplars: Effective ad copy; landing-page hero sections; brief sales emails; elevator pitches.

GENRE 750: Origin Story (compressed)
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Before — what life was like
    2. Spark — what started this
    3. Now — current state
  Description: 3-beat shape for short-form origin — brand, project, person, decision. Distinct from full Brand Origin Story by compression.
  Exemplars: About-page bios; LinkedIn summaries; brand intro videos; founder Twitter threads.

GENRE 751: Win (compressed)
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Stakes named
    2. Effort given
    3. Victory delivered
  Description: 3-beat shape for celebrating an achievement — sporting, professional, personal. Most basic triumph structure.
  Exemplars: "We did it!" announcements; sports recaps; project-win communications.

GENRE 752: Loss (compressed)
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Stakes named
    2. Effort given
    3. Defeat absorbed
  Description: 3-beat shape for facing an unwanted outcome — election loss, project failure, sporting defeat. Dignified acknowledgment.
  Exemplars: Concession statements; project-failure debriefs; public-loss acknowledgments.

GENRE 753: Comeback (compressed)
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Fall — what was lost or wrong
    2. Reckoning — work to come back
    3. Return — current state of being-back
  Description: 3-beat shape for short-form comeback narrative. Distinct from full comeback narrative by compression.
  Exemplars: "I'm back" posts; recovery-from-injury announcements; relaunch-after-failure communications.

GENRE 754: Explainer (3-beat)
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. What — the thing being explained
    2. Why — why it matters or exists
    3. How — how it works or what to do
  Description: 3-beat shape for short explanation — concept, product, process. Most basic explainer structure.
  Exemplars: Quick-explainer tweets; product-feature one-liners; concept introductions.

GENRE 755: Resignation (compressed)
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Position named
    2. Decision to leave
    3. Effective date
  Description: 3-beat shape for the most compressed resignation — distinct from formal resignation letter (615) by extreme brevity. Useful for verbal or text resignations.
  Exemplars: Brief resignation statements; verbal notice patterns; text-message resignations (controversial but common).

GENRE 756: Welcome (compressed)
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Recognition of arrival
    2. Bridge to existing community/space
    3. Open invitation to engage
  Description: 3-beat shape for welcoming — new hire, new resident, new member. Distinct from greeting by being directional (toward community).
  Exemplars: New-hire welcome messages; subscriber welcome emails; community-onboarding announcements.

GENRE 757: Refusal (compressed)
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Recognition of offer or request
    2. Polite decline
    3. Brief reason or alternative
  Description: 3-beat shape for saying no — preserving relationship while declining. Most basic boundary-without-rupture structure.
  Exemplars: "Thanks but no" messages; polite project declines; saying-no-to-meetings emails.

GENRE 758: Promise (compressed)
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Recognition of need or request
    2. Specific commitment
    3. Frame for accountability
  Description: 3-beat shape for making a promise — distinct from generic statement of intent by including the accountability frame.
  Exemplars: Manager commitments to direct reports; brand promises; relationship promises.

GENRE 759: Warning (compressed)
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Threat or danger named
    2. Specific risk to recipient
    3. Action to take
  Description: 3-beat shape for warning someone — about a person, situation, or risk. Distinguished from cautionary tale by compression and direct-recipient orientation.
  Exemplars: Warning messages between friends; institutional safety alerts; product-recall communications.

GENRE 760: Endorsement (compressed)
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Subject endorsed (person, product, idea)
    2. Specific reason for endorsement
    3. Recommendation that transfers credibility
  Description: 3-beat shape for short-form endorsement. Distinct from testimonial appeal (200) by being conversational rather than advertising.
  Exemplars: Personal recommendations; political endorsements (compressed); social-media praise posts.

GENRE 761: Excuse (compressed)
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Acknowledgment of the failure
    2. External reason given
    3. Implied or stated request for understanding
  Description: 3-beat shape for the explanation-of-failure that isn't quite an apology. Distinct from apology by externalizing cause; common but ethically loaded.
  Exemplars: Late-to-meeting explanations; missed-deadline messages; everyday social explanations.
  Notes: Useful as a recognized shape; ethical use depends on whether the external cause is genuine.

GENRE 762: Confidence-Building (compressed)
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Recognition of recipient's doubt
    2. Specific evidence of capability
    3. Affirmation of trust
  Description: 3-beat shape for building someone's confidence — colleague before a presentation, child before an exam. Distinct from generic encouragement by specific-evidence beat.
  Exemplars: Pre-game coach talks; mentor-to-mentee texts; manager-to-direct-report messages before stretch assignments.

GENRE 763: Boundary-Setting (compressed)
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Recognition of relationship or context
    2. Specific limit named
    3. Affirmation of continuing connection within new limit
  Description: 3-beat shape for setting a boundary — with family, partner, colleague. Distinct from refusal by being relational/ongoing rather than transactional.
  Exemplars: Therapeutic-conversation boundary statements; modern relational-health communications; workplace boundary messages.

GENRE 764: Question Held (compressed)
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Question raised
    2. Refusal to answer prematurely
    3. Question articulated more precisely
  Description: 3-beat compressed version of "The Held Question" (306) — for short-form contexts. Conversational shape for declining to resolve while staying engaged.
  Exemplars: Skilled therapist responses; Socratic conversation moves; thoughtful not-yet-answer statements.

GENRE 765: Quiet Defiance (compressed)
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Pressure or expectation named
    2. Refusal without escalation
    3. Continuation of one's path
  Description: 3-beat shape for refusing to comply without making it a fight. Distinct from confrontation; the resistance is in the *continuation*.
  Exemplars: Civil-disobedience statements; quiet-refusal-of-authority messages; "I respectfully disagree and will continue..." communications.

GENRE 766: Claim-and-Evidence (compressed)
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Claim asserted clearly
    2. Specific evidence offered
    3. Implication or call drawn
  Description: 3-beat shape for short argumentative content — distinct from full Toulmin model (130) by compression. Most basic persuasive structure.
  Exemplars: Effective short opinion pieces; brief persuasive emails; one-sentence-thesis arguments.

GENRE 767: Reframe (compressed)
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Common framing named
    2. Alternative framing offered
    3. Why the alternative serves better
  Description: 3-beat shape for changing how something is being thought about — distinct from hot take by being constructive rather than provocative.
  Exemplars: Coaching reframes; therapeutic reframes; brand-positioning shifts; political-message reframes.

GENRE 768: Public Stance (compressed)
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Issue named
    2. Position declared
    3. Reason offered
  Description: 3-beat shape for taking a public stand — on a political issue, social moment, or institutional question. Most basic structure for going on the record.
  Exemplars: CEO statements on social issues; celebrity stance-taking; institutional-position communications.

GENRE 769: Witness Statement (compressed)
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. What I saw or experienced (specifically)
    2. When and where (grounding)
    3. What it means for the record
  Description: 3-beat shape for testimony or eyewitness account — distinct from formal testimony by compression. Useful for journalism, social movements, accountability.
  Exemplars: Effective eyewitness tweets; brief court-testimony summaries; activist witness statements.

GENRE 770: Plea (compressed)
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Recognition of the audience's power to decide
    2. What is asked
    3. Why it matters
  Description: 3-beat shape for asking someone to do something they could refuse — reduce a sentence, donate, vote, forgive. Distinct from sales pitch by emphasis on the asker's vulnerability.
  Exemplars: Sentencing-statement pleas; fundraising messages; victim-impact statements; reconciliation requests.

---

# NGC ORIGINALS — More 3-Beat Communicative Shapes (Phase 7 continued)

GENRE 771: Pep Talk
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Recognition of the moment's stakes
    2. Affirmation of capability and prior wins
    3. Charge to action
  Description: Compressed motivational shape for pre-game, pre-presentation, pre-launch moments. Distinct from generic encouragement by its action-orienting close.
  Exemplars: Coach pep talks; manager rallies; pre-meeting team huddles; speech before the speech.

GENRE 772: Setting Expectations
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Context or relationship named
    2. Specific expectation stated
    3. Frame for accountability or check-in
  Description: 3-beat shape for clarifying what the recipient should anticipate. Distinct from promise by being mutual rather than one-directional.
  Exemplars: Manager-direct-report opening conversations; therapist-client first session; vendor-client kickoff messages.

GENRE 773: Calling Out
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Behavior or pattern named publicly
    2. Why it is unacceptable
    3. Demand for accountability or change
  Description: Public criticism shape — direct, on-the-record, intended to create pressure for change. Distinct from quiet conversation by public posture.
  Exemplars: Twitter callouts; institutional-accountability statements; political confrontations.

GENRE 774: Calling In
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Behavior or pattern noted privately
    2. Recognition of the relationship and shared values
    3. Invitation to reflect and do better
  Description: Private criticism shape — distinct from calling out by being relational rather than public. Aims at growth rather than pressure.
  Exemplars: Loretta Ross's calling-in framework; close-friendship corrections; mentor private feedback.

GENRE 775: Defending Someone
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Recognition that the person has been attacked or misrepresented
    2. Specific defense (what's actually true)
    3. Reaffirmation of the person's standing
  Description: 3-beat shape for stepping in for someone — colleague being misrepresented, friend in conflict, someone falsely accused.
  Exemplars: Workplace defense moments; friend-defending-friend interventions; public statements of solidarity.

GENRE 776: Asking for Help
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Specific situation acknowledged
    2. What is needed
    3. Why this person can help
  Description: 3-beat shape for requesting assistance — distinct from generic request by including the why-you specifically beat.
  Exemplars: Help requests in professional contexts; vulnerable asks; community-support solicitations.

GENRE 777: Asking for Advice
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Decision or situation framed
    2. Specific question asked
    3. What would be most useful from the advisor
  Description: 3-beat shape for soliciting advice — distinct from asking for help by being decision-oriented rather than action-oriented.
  Exemplars: Mentor consultations; "what would you do?" conversations; structured advice asks.

GENRE 778: Negotiation Opening
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Acknowledgment of mutual interest in agreement
    2. Initial position stated
    3. Willingness to understand other side
  Description: 3-beat shape for opening a negotiation. Frames the conversation as collaborative-problem-solving rather than zero-sum.
  Exemplars: Salary negotiation openings; vendor contract discussions; policy negotiations.

GENRE 779: Negotiation Close
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Summary of where both sides have moved
    2. Final terms named
    3. Confirmation and next steps
  Description: 3-beat shape for closing a deal. Locks in agreement clearly and orients toward implementation.
  Exemplars: Deal-closing messages; HR final-offer communications; sales-close conversations.

GENRE 780: Counter-Offer
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Acknowledgment of original offer's value
    2. Specific revision proposed
    3. Why the revision serves both parties
  Description: 3-beat shape for proposing alternative terms. Preserves relationship while renegotiating.
  Exemplars: Salary counter-offers; vendor counter-proposals; real-estate counter-offers.

GENRE 781: Walk-Back / Retraction
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Reference to prior statement or position
    2. Acknowledgment that it was wrong or premature
    3. Corrected position offered
  Description: 3-beat shape for correcting a public statement. Distinct from apology by focusing on the position rather than the harm.
  Exemplars: Corporate clarifications; public-figure walk-backs; corrections to prior reporting.

GENRE 782: Denial (compressed)
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Recognition of the accusation or claim
    2. Direct denial
    3. Reason or evidence
  Description: 3-beat shape for denying an accusation. Most basic structure for refuting publicly.
  Exemplars: Public-figure denials of wrongdoing; corporate denials of accusations; personal disputes.

GENRE 783: Doubling Down
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Recognition of pushback received
    2. Reaffirmation of original position
    3. Stronger commitment despite pressure
  Description: 3-beat shape for refusing to back down under pressure. Ethically loaded — useful when the original position deserves defense.
  Exemplars: Political statements after pushback; brand re-commitments; principled stands under criticism.

GENRE 784: Curiosity Expression
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Recognition of something unfamiliar or surprising
    2. Specific question
    3. Openness to whatever answer comes
  Description: 3-beat shape for genuine inquiry. Distinct from question-as-rhetorical-move by signaling actual receptivity.
  Exemplars: Genuine curiosity in conversation; ethnographic-mode questions; child-like inquiry as adult communication.

GENRE 785: Status Update
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. What has been done since last update
    2. What is in progress
    3. What is blocked or coming next
  Description: 3-beat shape for project status — distinct from progress report by being conversational and current. Standard agile/operating cadence.
  Exemplars: Standup meeting updates; project-status emails; progress messages to stakeholders.

GENRE 786: Reminder
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Reference to prior commitment or context
    2. What needs to happen
    3. By when
  Description: 3-beat shape for nudging without nagging. Most basic structure for productive follow-up.
  Exemplars: Calendar reminders; gentle follow-up emails; deadline nudges.

GENRE 787: Heads-Up
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Recognition of relevance to recipient
    2. Specific information shared
    3. What recipient might want to do
  Description: 3-beat shape for proactive information-sharing. Distinct from generic FYI by including recipient-action beat.
  Exemplars: "FYI before this hits your inbox..." messages; insider warning communications; courtesy-update conversations.

GENRE 788: Synthesis
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Recognition of multiple positions or inputs
    2. Pattern or common ground identified
    3. Unified synthesis or proposed direction
  Description: 3-beat shape for pulling together a discussion's threads. The structural payoff is the integration, not new content.
  Exemplars: Meeting-closing summaries; thought-leadership integrations; mediator interventions.

GENRE 789: Compliment (compressed)
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Specific quality or action observed
    2. What it conveys about the person
    3. Affirmation
  Description: 3-beat shape for genuine praise — distinct from generic compliment by being specific and inferential.
  Exemplars: Effective performance-feedback praise; relational compliments; mentor-affirmation messages.

GENRE 790: Constructive Criticism
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Recognition of strength and intent
    2. Specific concern with example
    3. Suggested improvement
  Description: 3-beat shape for feedback that grows rather than diminishes. Distinct from calling out by being private and developmental.
  Exemplars: Manager feedback to direct reports; teacher feedback on student work; peer-review comments.

GENRE 791: Validation (compressed)
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Acknowledgment of what the other is feeling or experiencing
    2. Affirmation that the response makes sense
    3. Continuation of presence without fixing
  Description: 3-beat shape for emotional validation — distinct from advice by deliberately not fixing. Therapeutic and relational core skill.
  Exemplars: Therapist responses; effective parental validation; close-friendship support messages.

GENRE 792: Empathic Reflection
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Listen to what the other has said
    2. Reflect back what was heard
    3. Open question or invitation to continue
  Description: 3-beat shape for active listening. Distinct from validation by reflecting content as well as feeling.
  Exemplars: Coaching conversation moves; therapeutic listening; effective conflict-mediation patterns.

GENRE 793: Setting Context
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Recognition that the recipient may not know the background
    2. Specific context shared
    3. Why the context matters now
  Description: 3-beat shape for orienting someone before a request, decision, or conversation. Most basic structure for "let me bring you up to speed."
  Exemplars: Pre-meeting briefings; "before we get into this..." conversations; new-to-the-team onboarding moments.

GENRE 794: Diffusing Tension
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Acknowledgment of the heated moment
    2. Specific de-escalating offer or naming
    3. Forward path that lowers temperature
  Description: 3-beat shape for stepping into and reducing conflict. Distinct from validation by being situational rather than sustained.
  Exemplars: Mediator interventions; effective de-escalation in arguments; conflict-resolution moves.

GENRE 795: Acknowledging Someone's Win
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Specific recognition of the achievement
    2. What it took to get there
    3. Genuine celebration without diminishment
  Description: 3-beat shape for honoring someone else's success — distinct from generic congratulations by recognizing the work.
  Exemplars: Promotion congratulations; project-completion celebrations; relationship-milestone acknowledgments.

GENRE 796: Sharing Good News
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Setup or context
    2. The news itself
    3. What it means or what's next
  Description: 3-beat shape for sharing a positive development. Distinct from win by being broader (life events, not just achievements).
  Exemplars: Engagement announcements; promotion shares; pregnancy reveals; project-launch posts.

GENRE 797: Sharing Difficult News
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Acknowledgment that this is hard to share
    2. The news, stated clearly
    3. What support or response is needed
  Description: 3-beat shape for delivering bad or hard news — distinct from confession by being external (not self-disclosure) rather than internal.
  Exemplars: Layoff notifications; medical-news shares with family; loss-of-loved-one announcements.

GENRE 798: Inviting to Event
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Event named with stakes
    2. Specific invitation to recipient
    3. RSVP frame
  Description: 3-beat shape for inviting — distinct from announcement by being recipient-specific.
  Exemplars: Wedding invitations; conference invitations; personal-event invitations.

GENRE 799: Declining an Invitation
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Acknowledgment of the invitation
    2. Decline with brief reason (or none)
    3. Affirmation of continuing relationship
  Description: 3-beat shape for declining gracefully. Distinct from refusal by being relational and warm.
  Exemplars: Polite RSVP-no messages; declining work-event invitations while preserving relationships.

GENRE 800: Confirming Attendance
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Acknowledgment of the invitation
    2. Confirmation of attendance
    3. Anticipation or specific question about logistics
  Description: 3-beat shape for accepting and engaging with an invitation. Specific and warm rather than generic.
  Exemplars: RSVP-yes messages; meeting confirmations; event acknowledgments.

GENRE 801: Re-engagement After Silence
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Recognition of the time that has passed
    2. Specific reason for reaching out now
    3. Open invitation to reconnect
  Description: 3-beat shape for restarting a dormant relationship — friendship, professional connection, family contact. Acknowledges the gap honestly.
  Exemplars: "It's been a while..." messages; LinkedIn reconnection notes; family-reach-out communications.

GENRE 802: Disagreement With Respect
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Acknowledgment of the other's position
    2. Specific point of disagreement
    3. Affirmation of continuing relationship
  Description: 3-beat shape for disagreeing without rupture. Distinct from refutation by emphasizing relational continuity.
  Exemplars: Mature professional disagreements; family political conversations; principled-but-civil debates.

GENRE 803: Self-Deprecation Done Well
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Wry recognition of own limitation
    2. Specific moment that exemplifies it
    3. Lesson, laugh, or shared truth
  Description: 3-beat shape for owning a flaw with humor — without self-flagellation. Builds connection through honesty.
  Exemplars: Effective comedic memoir; conversational charm; brand "we screwed up" honesty.

GENRE 804: Vulnerability Share
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Recognition that this is hard to share
    2. The vulnerable disclosure
    3. What is being asked of the listener (often: just to receive)
  Description: 3-beat shape for sharing emotional truth. Distinct from confession by being current and continuing rather than retrospective.
  Exemplars: Therapeutic disclosures; intimate-relationship vulnerability; brave-public-statement vulnerabilities.

GENRE 805: Curiosity-Invitation
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Recognition that the other might be interested
    2. Specific opening offered
    3. Easy yes-or-no with no pressure
  Description: 3-beat shape for inviting someone into a conversation, project, or experience without forcing. Low-pressure outreach.
  Exemplars: Casual connection invitations; "would you ever want to..." messages; soft-pitch communications.

GENRE 806: Wisdom-Passing
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Recognition of the recipient's question or situation
    2. Specific lived experience offered
    3. Open frame — take what's useful
  Description: 3-beat shape for offering hard-won insight — distinct from advice by being more about disclosure than prescription.
  Exemplars: Elder-to-younger-generation conversations; mentor disclosure of past mistakes; veteran professionals' counsel to newcomers.

GENRE 807: Memory-Sharing
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Specific memory invoked
    2. Detail rendered
    3. Connection to present moment or recipient
  Description: 3-beat shape for sharing a memory — at a funeral, anniversary, or quiet moment. Distinct from storytelling by intimacy and present-tense relevance.
  Exemplars: Eulogy memory-passages; anniversary reflections; intimate memory-shares between long-known people.

GENRE 808: Acknowledging the Elephant in the Room
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Recognition that something unspoken is in the room
    2. Direct naming of it
    3. Invitation to address it together
  Description: 3-beat shape for breaking conversational avoidance. The structural payoff is the relief of the named.
  Exemplars: Therapy-breakthrough moments; team-meeting honesty; family-gathering courage.

GENRE 809: "How Can I Help"
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Recognition of the other's situation
    2. Direct offer of help
    3. Specific frame — what kinds of help are available
  Description: 3-beat shape for offering support without overstepping. Distinct from generic offer by being concrete about what's on offer.
  Exemplars: Effective grief-support messages; team-support offers; community mutual-aid communications.

GENRE 810: Inquiry into Wellbeing
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Recognition that something has happened or might be happening
    2. Specific question about wellbeing
    3. Acknowledgment that no answer is required
  Description: 3-beat shape for the genuine "are you okay?" message. Distinct from generic check-in by specificity and pressure-release.
  Exemplars: Friend check-ins after difficulty; manager wellness inquiries; thoughtful "thinking of you" messages.

GENRE 811: Forgiveness-Asked
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Acknowledgment of the harm
    2. Specific request for forgiveness
    3. Recognition that the answer is not owed
  Description: 3-beat shape for asking forgiveness. Distinct from apology by explicitly seeking absolution while acknowledging it's not earned.
  Exemplars: Reconciliation-conversation requests; restorative-justice statements; intimate-relationship repair attempts.

GENRE 812: Forgiveness-Granted
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Acknowledgment of what was done
    2. Decision to forgive
    3. Frame for what comes next
  Description: 3-beat shape for granting forgiveness — distinct from generic acceptance by including the forward frame.
  Exemplars: Truth-and-Reconciliation testimony responses; intimate-relationship forgiveness moments; therapeutic-letter exercises.

GENRE 813: Distance / Space-Asked
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Recognition of the relationship
    2. Specific need for distance or break
    3. Frame for what continues or how to reconnect
  Description: 3-beat shape for asking for space without rupture. Distinct from boundary-setting by being temporary.
  Exemplars: Relationship pause communications; family-space requests; therapeutic-break discussions.

GENRE 814: Coming Closer
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Recognition that distance has existed
    2. Specific desire to come closer
    3. Specific opening offered
  Description: 3-beat shape for moving toward someone after distance. Distinct from re-engagement by being more intimate.
  Exemplars: "I miss you" messages done well; reconciliation-initiation communications; tender bid-for-closeness.

GENRE 815: Patience-Asked
  Tradition: Universal (NGC-original)
  Laws (beats):
    1. Acknowledgment that the other is waiting
    2. Specific request for patience
    3. Realistic frame for when resolution is coming
  Description: 3-beat shape for asking for time. Distinct from generic update by being explicit about the request.
  Exemplars: Project-delay communications; "I'm working on it" messages with specifics; relationship "I need time" conversations.
